Leaf by leaf it runs from 121% of the Book (the Invocation) to 166% (the Epilogue), apart from Of This Book with its note. Eight leaves are a little over 150% (I.4, II.1, V.1, V.5, V.7, VI.1, VI.3 and the Epilogue), where the Book leans on earlier tales and the Plain explains them. v3.0’s Plain ran 84.8% of the Book by §1.10’s count. There are 29 when lines, one wherever the Book’s leaf has a dateline, of 15 to 70 words. The markup checker passes (wf13/_markup_cmp_final.py): the skeleton of headings and rules is the Book’s (52), and the comments (78), fences (34), native keys (15, every native block byte for byte), blots (12), chips (3) and tokens (2) stand in the same order as in v3.0’s Plain, with no section different.

The word-by-word romanisation

Every line in a Romanisation fold of the Original tab now stands word over word: each romanised Orrowen word in italic, with its English beneath it in a small, quieter type (wf13/orig/build_panes.py, rom_p). The fold is closed until the reader opens it. The line still wraps as a line of words, each word keeps its punctuation and a pair-name its lintel, and a screen reader hears the romanised line once and then its English, word by word. Only these folds carry the gloss.

The Book (Of This Book)On the wall I see the banner lift, in a wind I hear not.
The romanisationUm et hald veskym et movenn ul memmyl, ul helvhoss sa nath hunnom.
The analyzer’s draftupon(+S) · the · wall · see-1SG · the · banner · in(+N) · N·lifting · in(+N) · wind · REL(+S) · NEG(+N) · hear-1SG
On the pageon · the · wall · I see · the · banner · in · lifting · in · wind · that · not · I hear

How the gloss was made

  1. The analyzer’s drafts. wf13/extract_rom.py took every romanised line from the Original’s folds and grouped the lines by the twenty units that set them down: S01 to S11 and S03x (the Title under the new capstone, I.4) for the stone and the Book’s furniture, and W01 to W08 for the wood. The Orrowen analyzer gave each word a mechanical reading as its draft (wf13/gloss_in/). Every word had one, and none was unknown.
  2. The shared table. wf13/gloss_table.md is the one rulebook for every gloss writer, so that a word reads the same on every page. Its authority runs the lexicon first, then the grammar, then the analyzer, whose draft is only a draft. Its rules: one gloss to a word, in the sense the paragraph needs (keth is “hold” or “know”); plain modern English, in lower case except where the Book’s English has a capital; literal, so an idiom shows through its own words (ul vimm et hellur is “in” “low” “the” “hall”, not “under the hall”); no mutations shown; parentheses only for the particles English has no word for, “(past)”, “(future)” and “(question)”, and for a pair-name, “Halyna (the two)”; a person by the Book’s spelling (Wik is “Wick”), and a place of two words by its words (“Keep” “Riven” for Rivenkeep). No name is taken apart: a wood name such as Aelthae is glossed as itself, as the Plain keeps it whole. The table also lists where the analyzer goes wrong (Kael is always the man, never “tally-notch”).
  3. Glossed, then verified. The twenty units were glossed from their drafts and the table (wf13/gloss/), and each was then verified against the lexicon and the Book’s English. In the eight units that kept their copy from before that check, it changed 72 words in 63 lines. Of the 25,734 words in distinct lines, the writers kept the analyzer’s word for 9,969 and rewrote the rest: its tags into English (see-1SG to “I see”), and its sense where the sentence needs another (lern from “face” to “side”).

The counts

wf13/check_gloss.py passes for all twenty units: 1,288 lines, 0 problems. Every line has its gloss, with exactly the analyzer’s words in their order, and no gloss is empty, a question mark or a raw tag. The page builder stops on any line that lacks its gloss or has other words, and it set 1,288 lines word over word, 26,532 words. One fold line holds no word and stands as it was.

UnitsLinesWords glossed
Stone and the Book’s furniture (S01 to S11, S03x)1,18725,434
Wood (W01 to W08)1011,098
All twenty1,28826,532

The 1,288 lines hold 1,133 distinct ones. The 155 repeats, most of them the hearth’s answer and the teller’s closing line, are glossed once and read alike everywhere.

Still open, for Jack

  1. The Original now gives English, word by word, on the story page. In the game the Original tab translates nothing, for the player who decodes (§5.8), and the hands are translated rung by rung on the unlock ladder (§5.7). On the page the gloss sits closed inside each fold. Whether the game’s reader shows it as the page does, or gives a line’s English only with its rung, is Jack’s call.
  2. From the Plain’s editor (wf13/plain_notes.md, For Jack), each applied and each easy to undo. The Fenholm saying has newest course in III.2 and VI.1 where the Book has last course, so that a newcomer does not read last as final. The five wood leaves that carry no pair-name in v3.0’s Plain (IV.4, IV.6, V.3, V.4, V.6) name Halvard and Rhyna in full. II.2’s headnote says the word for the wood’s people came much later, a small look ahead that can go if the note box stays hidden until VI.3 ripens.
  3. Jack’s critique of the tales is still to come (his third note).

2What v2 changed, and what of it stands

v2.0 (2026-10-01) applied Jack’s twenty-six notes of 2026-09-30. Most of what it settled still stands in v3: the order of the Book, the clock, the pair-names under the lintel, the runners, the marks, the oils, the staff, the twelve deaths, the half-cloak and the wood that knows only what the wood could know. What it got wrong is what Jack turned down on 2026-10-02: the long joined sentences, the length, the chronicle frame, Brenn’s blows and the custom of burning. This section records each note of 2026-09-30 as the v3 Book now holds it.

2.1 · The notes of 2026-09-30 that set the v2 work

  • The impact (J1). “The stories have lost some of their impact moments. It feels like you are just reading a description of the text, not an Epic Poem. This is supposed to keep the attention of all listeners of the tale — young and old, first time or 100th time. This should be formatted like the Odyssey but story building like JRR Tolkien. Give the stories feeling, then translate them into the other language.” Stands, and v3 answers it better: every tale has one halt told close and its strike sentences on their own paragraphs (§1.4).
  • The form. Offered verse lines, Jack chose prose: “I still like the heightened prose.” Stands. The paragraph is the unit, and the only verse in the Book is the songs. “Can the heightened prose be made to be read aloud?” is superseded by K4: “I don’t care is it is easier to read aloud.”
  • The register. “The new text should NOT be plain words. The words should be flowery when necessary. The original text should be just a bit hard to read for someone used to modern English. I like the longer sentences joined together. I like that there is a lot of difference between The Book and the Plain Words.” Superseded for sentence length by K4: the v2.0 run-ons failed, and the Book is hard through old grammar, not through length. Standing: not plain words, hard to read for someone used to modern English, and a lot of difference between The Book and the Plain Words, which the HEAVY register and the retold Plain now deliver (§1.2, §1.10).

2.2 · Jack’s notes of 2026-09-30, note by note, as the v3 Book holds them

J1 · Impact moments; an epic, not a description of one; the Odyssey’s form and Tolkien’s building; feeling first, then the other tongue.
  • The strike lines of v3, in Book order: He tore it down the back. (I.1) · There was no silence. (I.2) · The Captain standeth still. (I.3) · It was the mark of Aldwena. and Four hands laid it in two. (I.4) · In the morning she did not wake. (I.5) · It was their own. and No one heard it fall. (II.1) · She woke beside him. (II.3) · The ice held. (III.2) · ▒▒▒▒ kicked it aside., We obeyed. and Tarnel did not wake. (IV.3) · And we were the one his foot found. (IV.4) · His father was Daveth. and The tune stopped. (IV.5) · Then we turned. (IV.6) · I let go. (V.1) · It said Burn. and The broken grain met. (V.2) · His were. (V.5, Count II) · She was gone., Ye cannot tell. and “One,” he said. (V.7, Part I) · Pellow lay dead at the parapet. (V.7, Part II) · The ice took Hesk too. (VI.1) · It was green. and The wood took it. (VI.3) · It was the last of his half. and I knew the bite. (the Epilogue).
  • From the Odyssey, the format, unchanged: Books with their Arguments; the Invocation as the proem; the story begun in the middle, at the banner, reaching back through Books Two to Four and coming forward through Five and Six; catalogues (the roll of the Twelve, the havens twice, the cargo count, the carvings, the Thrones, the roll of the dead); epithets and formula lines; songs set in the tales; the law of the guest and its breaking (IV.3, IV.4) and its answer (the kneeling, VI.3); two homecomings, ours (VI.1) and theirs (VI.2, VI.3); recognition by token (the boat’s stem, a chisel’s bite, the warden’s long sleeves); a bard inside the story (Seren); listeners who interrupt (the child of II.2, the child of IV.4, the children of II.3, the child of the Last Note).
  • From Tolkien, the methods and never the sentences, unchanged: close scenes breaking the annal distance; genealogies (Aldwena, then Rhyna; the quarry-wardens, then Halvard; Elspet, then Daveth, then Merrick; Ebba, then Marl, then the fisher-girl); many names for one thing (the grey, the fog, the breath, the Mystlands); found documents (the founders’ leaf, the rolls, the warden’s letter, Brenn’s confession, the youngest apprentice’s leaf); the long defeat, in which every victory costs a strip of the cloak; and the turn of grace no one looked for, the green sliver.
  • Feeling first, then the other tongue. The English is written first, with full feeling. The Original must now be set down from v3 (K2; §1.11).
  • v2.0’s bent (more impact moments than the outline’s one to three, and tales past 900 words) is resolved: one halt to a tale, and every tale within its ceiling.
J2 · Show that Halyna is one name for two people, simply, and never as a “they/them” reading.
  • The lintel. Every pair-name is set under one thin stroke the width of the name, as a capstone lies across two posts. The source marks it {{Halyna}} and the builder draws the stroke. Seren says why once, in the foreword: So write I every bonded pair, under one stroke, as a lintel on two posts. It is one name, and it is the name of two. II.3 has already seeded the picture: the pair-name is made as a gate is made of two posts and the stone laid across them.
  • The English. A pair-name always takes a plural verb (Halyna are, Halyna were silent), and in its own mouth says we two. Changed in v3: v2.0 showed two people at the first mention in every tale with he and she, the two of them or both, and used those phrases 52 times in all. v3 uses neither phrase once: The lintel says two, and the verb says two. Nothing more is needed (craft rule 20). When one of them acts alone, the Book uses the cradle-name and he or she. Husband and wife is said in I.2 and II.3. No singular they is used for anyone.
  • Six pair-names: Halyna (Halvard and Rhyna), Aldwena (the first builders), Idrenna (the eldest pair), Orvenna (Ormund and Penna, the wall-masters), Enrella (Enno and Della, the young pair) and Wendhessa (the archivists of Eldhythe). The cradle-names of Aldwena (Aldun and Bena), of Idrenna (Idlan and Denna) and of Wendhessa (Wendel and Tessa) live only in these notes.
J3 · Make clear where each Book and tale stands in time.
  • The hinge is still the banner (§3.1), and the whole clock is in §3.
  • Changed in v3: v2.0’s two-clause datelines in capitals (LAID IN THE THIRD WINTER… · OF ONE DAY AT THE OUTERMOST OUTPOST…) were the history-book voice. A v3 dateline names only when the tale was laid, in eight words or fewer, in plain italic (The third winter, in the Tide of Remembering.). When its matter happened is told by the tale itself, or by these notes (§3.5).
  • An Argument under every Book, one line that spoils nothing (§1.4).
  • Every tale’s first sentence of story gives an hour.
J4 · The explaining that interrupts the story is needed, but must be done better.
  • v3 goes further than v2.0. Explanation travels in deed (the Rite read off the slate in halves while Seren forgets her bucket; the mark drawn once in the ash), in a line of speech, in a song (the masons’ chant names the three parts of the siege day; the dawn drill carries the three calls), in the foreword said once, or out of the Book into these notes (§2.4).
  • II.3 is the one tale that may teach, and it teaches through the children: the small girl’s question, Enno’s jest, the boy who counts twelve on his fingers and runs out of fingers, and Idrenna, who do not nod, and then do.
  • Nothing is explained at or near a halt.
J5 · The poems are wonderful: keep them.
  • Every verse stands letter for letter: The Lay of the Torn Cloak (I.1); Two at the Gate (I.2, and at Halyna’s grave in the Epilogue); The Grey Boat with its two unsung lines (IV.5); The Song of the Years (V.6); The Two Homes (VI.3).
  • The short verses v2.0 added stand too: the masons’ work-chant (I.2), the True Men’s dawn drill of the three calls (I.3), and Rhyna’s mother’s cradle-rhyme of Aldwena’s line (I.4). Idrenna’s prayer for the dead, in prose and in two halves, is told whole once (V.7 Part I) and told without its words at Ebba’s grave (I.5). Its address is now God, never Lord (§7.2 call 13).
J6 · People die in every epic; invest in them so the dying lands.
  • Twelve deaths, D1 to D12 (§3.8). Each is planted as a habit, a want or a promise, told whole once, cold, and remembered for its good.
  • The half-cloak. The Captain wears the unwritten half of the torn cloak and cuts a hand’s breadth from it for the wall’s dead laid at this hearth, bound on a pike over the grave. Seven strips are told in the Book (Jory, Corlen, Wick, Enrella, Pellow, Ebba, Halyna), and Hesk’s is a call (§7.2 call 3). The last goes to Halyna’s gate, and he walks into the seventh winter in a plain grey cloak.
  • Changed in v3: v2.0 followed every death with three things, one of them the hood. In v3 the hood goes back twice in the whole Book (§1.4), and the strip carries the rest.
J7 · The runner needs a purpose, and a horn cannot carry that far.
  • The horn is gone everywhere. The Captain calls with his own voice at the battery’s side, and a man can stand in only one place: “Naught may change that mind but his own voice at my side, lad, and a man can stand but in one place.” (Kael to Hale, I.3).
  • The True Men fight their batteries by the mind he gave each at dawn: where to watch, what shot to lay, and when to hold.
  • The runners are his eyes. A runner’s word is three words, like a chisel-cut: “Hull hangeth back.” · “Marks on the wood.” · “They are looking.” · “They are not green.”
  • The runners’ oath and stone. “What I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else.” New in v3: Every haven had its runners’ stone. Ours we made the first week, of a fallen block by the inner gate, and on it we sware the old oath. Brenn swore it at fifteen with mine hand flat on the runners’ stone. The morning after IV.3 the wall’s runners swear it again and add four words: Not as Brenn carried.
J8 · The mark is the Stonwryt: a symbol every bonded pair owns and holds sacred.
  • The seers read it at the cradle; the guild gives it at the sealing, a name for the mouth, and a mark for the stone; No hand but theirs may cut it, and no other pair will break it. (II.3). Jack’s words are set as a quoted formula: The Stonwryt the masters call it: a vow sealed in living rock, earned by toil and skill, and no gold buyeth it. Master-pairs struck coin under their marks: trust, sealed in stone. (II.1).
  • No pair cuts or breaks another pair’s mark, which is why Brenn’s bundle waited under Wendhessa’s seal until Idrenna gave leave. The unbonded have no mark (I have no mark. Never shall I cut one.).
  • Four marks recur and can be matched by the player (§5.6): Aldwena’s, Halyna’s, Enrella’s and Wendhessa’s broken seal.
  • It is held sacred, but it is not a sign of the faith. No pair’s mark is drawn as a cross.
J9 · Halyna had to make the oils for the anointing, and that is part of what they found.
  • Three oils the Rite must have, that none living had made. The build by three (I.4):
    • cedar, from fallen heartwood only, at the stand where the first staff was cut; A living tree would they not fell. Stannard knocks and listens: “I know which of these will hold.” They render it at this hearth, and on a wet night its stones breathe it out yet;
    • granite-bloom: Bloom the masons call the pale crust on worked stone untended for lives of men, for it flowereth where none come. The children scrape it, Seren among them, as gleaners along a stubble-field, and My fingers were raw with it. (Unsaid: the forgetting that broke the Keep grew the strength of its Commander.);
    • myrrh, which the mountain cannot give: Idrenna carried one flask the whole road for the guild’s dead, and On the pass the ground would not take them. Rhyna warms it into the cedar. I wist for whom it had been carried.
  • So the Rite falls on the fourteenth day, by a thin sun.
J10 · Why was the staff left behind? Generations of staffs, with Halyna finding the original.
  • The law of the staff, read off the slate in halves: “A staff is cut for its work, and leaveth not its work,” / “and when the work is done, the staff is laid down in it.” The founders laid it in the black chest when they went down, as masons lay by their tools at dusk.
  • Jack’s description, as a quoted formula: The slate nameth it so: a rod of polished wood capped with the finest polished ashlar, borne by every overseer of a great Stonewright work since the founding. Then It was the first staff of all.
  • In every falling haven the masters leaned its staff against the falling wall (as a woman banketh the fire on her last night in her mother’s house, the same as every night), and all ten were lost. The Captain leans the first in the gatehouse corner, for his work was the wall and the war. Jack’s line: If you doubt the tale, go and look at the dust on it. In the seventh spring it is gone, a clean stroke in the dust; The work is done. I know the stair that goes no more. · I have not gone down to look.
J11 · Every story fits the others like a puzzle piece.

The locks are in §4.

J12 · Orrowen has the rhythm of a line just short of song. Seren enjoys the tongue, and her craft is words. Plain Words unwinds it.
  • I love our tongue. Seren sets each tale as masons set a course, and says each word three times to hear whether it will hold. She lingers on point (I love that word.), on bloom, shore and Naelear, and a word fails her: I tried that word under my tongue, and it gave, like mortar gone to sand. (fortune, IV.1).
  • Plain Words no longer unwinds the Book: it retells it (§1.10). The Orrowen measure, the dual and the consonant rhyme are the Tongues team’s (§5.8, §5.14).
J13 · Halyna never speak of themselves in the third person.
  • They say we two, twenty times in v3. When one speaks of the other it is he, she or the cradle-name.
  • Every stone tale Halyna once told of themselves is Seren’s. II.3 is the one stone tale still theirs.
  • Bent, as before: Rhyna says So were we two sealed, and named Halyna. once in II.3, as a name received (§7.2 call 21).
J14 · The seers read a bond God made before birth; they do not choose it.
  • II.3: We hold that God made each pair one ere either drew breath. The seers go among the cradles with a lamp, and bend low, and read what was written before ever we were born. They choose not. And: the bond is made before the cradle, read in the cradle, and made whole only at the sealing. Idrenna’s prayer says it again in rite: “God, thou madest these two one ere either drew breath.”
J15 · We know of only one bonded pair, and Halyna do too much. They should be the discoverers of the stories Seren writes.
  • Halyna keep what is theirs by right: the cry, the finds, the making of the oils, the reading aloud of Brenn, the knowings, their own mark, the telling of II.3, and the kneeling at the front of the line. v3 moves two more deeds to other hands: the True Men raise the new capstone into the gate (I.4), and Halyna together, not Halvard alone, cut Halvard’s name in the lintel (the Epilogue). Their finds and the deeds of others are in §3.9.
J16 · We remember is like Amen.
  • After every stone tale the whole hearth answers And the hearth said: We remember. (33 times in v3, the Invocation’s included). It never varies.
  • It comes out of the first words of the Title. Old Ebba of Fenholm said it first, the first night the hearth asked a tale, into the quiet (the foreword). Most nights after, she began it a breath before the rest. After her death the hearth waits a breath for her (I.5, and at Fenholm’s fire in VI.1).
  • A wood leaf is answered with silence, And no one answered., until VI.3, when her great-granddaughter says “We remember.” to the wood first, and the hearth follows.
  • It is never said over the warden. It is first said over a killing by our own after IV.3, and the rest were slow to follow. But they followed. It is never a joke, a Whisper or store art. New in v3: it is a vow as well as an answer (§1.9).
J17 · The Guest’s killing is brutal, and his broken body is thrown into the boat.
  • Stands, with K7’s change. The post’s men take up staves, stones of the shingle and the boat-poles; One of them took up my pole; The blows I counted. I count them yet.; When he lay still, ▒▒▒▒ screamed on, and some went on.; Both his arms they broke: the one that had held the warden’s neck so gently, and the one that had held the blade.; By the heels they drew him down the shingle, and his head went on every stone.; and they cast him into his boat as a fisher casteth a torn net into the bilge. Brenn strikes no blow (§1.7). The wood holds his last hour by his blood (IV.4) and the boat holds the throwing (IV.6).
J18 · The burning of the boat has a reason.
  • Superseded by K8. The reason is the warden’s own: to hide the deed, Leave naught to show. v2.0’s custom of the coast is gone from the Book (§1.8).
J19 · Brenn is a runner who must take the report to the Council.
  • Stands. Brenn was the outpost’s runner, sworn at fifteen. On the morrow, ere any woke, I picked the stone out of the dust. Why I did it, I know not. At his door ▒▒▒▒ gave me the letter. So the gift and the lie went into one pack, and I ran it. He runs a day and a night, saying the oath every mile. At Eldhythe an old clerk asks “Is it so?”, and Brenn answers “It is written.” He confesses in his old age to Wendhessa and dies as a man setteth down a load.
J20 · The warden’s name is blotted out with ▒▒▒▒.
  • Stands. Always exactly four marks, never with an ending, twelve times in v3 (once in IV.1, eleven times in IV.3). The guild buried him under his name. The warden’s name it cut away. IV.1 shows the blot without a word of why, and Rhyna lays her finger on it and takes it away; IV.3’s annal says why.
  • At every blot Halyna were silent, so the hearth hears a silence where the name was. In the wood he never had one: he is the master of that house, the host.
J21 · The boat with the body rams the Shorelanders’ ships in its fury: two boats, though the ship-masters say twenty.
  • Stands. Twice on that road, where the breath is thin, hulls of the shore-men lay across our way. … We went into them burning, low, where they were heaviest. (IV.6). In IV.5 Elspet counts the boats on her fingers and lays her hand flat on the board: There were two boats. / Now the ship-masters say twenty. IV.1 strikes two timber-boats off a cargo-list, lost at the edge.
  • Changed in v3: Seren no longer remarks on it. v2.0’s seam (The wood says two. The ship-masters say twenty. I have kept both.) is cut; the two facing leaves agree in silence, and the Invocation keeps both every night.
J22 · The few sailors who survived the ramming started the rumours.
  • Stands, and now carries more: with no custom, the rumours are the whole root of the coast’s fear of grey boats (§1.8). Three came home; Daveth came home on a spar; All that autumn the three told it, and never again set foot in a boat. The outpost men swore in their cups. The shore laughed at the three from the spar. The written lie swallowed the spoken truth: And who will argue with writing? Daveth drank on his tale, and the harbour drowned him a stone’s throw from his mother’s house; his son is Merrick.
  • The fear helped end the felling: After the year of the burning boat no crew would row within sight of the grey, and the rolls say not why. (IV.1), against the wood’s In those same summers the iron came no more to the edge. The grove had not waited to see it. (V.4).
J23 · The stone and the grain read very differently. Keep that.
  • Stands, by a new road. Both now carry old grammar, so the difference is in who speaks: the stone in the tellers’ own voices, curt or weighing or circling; the wood in the eldest grammar of the Book, object first, in the wood’s we, with its own names for things and its ring formula; and above each wood leaf, Halyna’s fragments, which are like nothing else in the Book. Seren’s headnotes are stone even on wood leaves, so the seam stays visible.
J24 · The grain holds only what the wood could know.
  • Stands, and is tightened (§1.5). What moved out of the wood’s mouth in v2.0 stays out, and v3 moves four more things.
Wood leafTaken out of the wood’s mouthVersion
II.2the Shore’s names for the mist and the pillars; no one lies under a silverbarkv2.0 (none lie beneath a silver elder)

  • Where the two sides disagree, the Book keeps both without remarking on it. Glasspire’s night stays unsolved for ever: some who fled saw a shadow in the fog, Pellow saw nothing, and the wood says No throne sailed the night the spire fell. Of that night the grain hath naught.
J25 · The common origin of the two tongues is hinted at, never revealed. The parting stays untold. No cross-sign is a holy sign.
  • One hint reaches the hearth: ael. In V.7 Part I, The carving named that Tide Aelthae. That night I knew its first half. Ael I had written before, in Ael’thar. I have written the name, and I will not write what I thought. Changed in v3: no gloss is given. The reader joins it alone from three meetings: Aelrhen … bond-to-stone (IV.4), Aelvaren … the Bearer of the Binding (IV.6), and the Joined Tide set against Aelthae.
  • The first builders had known war. Against what, the slates say not. Never tied to the parting.
  • The Bar Before keeps its drawn shape, but it is never holy, and no pair’s mark is drawn as a cross. The warden’s half-mark is the half of something, cut off in the middle.
J26 · The story is a standalone document; how it was made and how it enters the game go into a separate NOTES document.
  • Stands. The Book begins with its title page and holds no writer’s note, no marker’s explanation and no Translation, unlocked by line. K3 adds: the copy Jack shares is that whole standalone story (§1.11).

2.3 · The v2 reordering, and the anchors

v2.0’s changes to the order stand in v3:

  • Book Three holds only the havens’ raising and fall: III.1 Of Their Raising and III.2 Of Their Fall, which v1.3 had as one tale in two parts.
  • The Homecomings and the Thrones moved to Book Six, the Book of the Homecomings, where they face each other: VI.1 (v1.3’s homecoming captions, now each True Man’s own entry) and VI.2 (v1.3’s III.2). v1.3’s VI.1, the green wood, is VI.3.
  • Anchors for the builder and for links from other documents. A tale that keeps its number keeps its anchor (t-I-1 and so on). Links that pointed at v1.3’s III.2 (the Thrones) belong at t-VI-2, and links at v1.3’s VI.1 (the green wood) at t-VI-3.
  • The facing spreads are v2.0’s (§4.6). In v3 the Contents names each one (facing II.2), since a headnote no longer holds a rule of the Book.
  • The fallback (outline §12, call 1) is v1.3’s order with only the Arguments and datelines added.

2.4 · What moved out of the Book and into these notes

Nothing below is said in any tale. Each item has its home in this document.

  • The mechanics (§5.10, §5.11, and the fleet design): a battery is a gun group; the three Fight verbs; Command Time and its numbers, including the twenty-four-hour day, which v3 also takes out of I.3; the twelve-gun fit; every gun hauled is a course not laid (said in the Book only as Orvenna’s two halves); the twelvefold count of a pair’s work (the Book keeps only The work of a pair is the work of twelve, as the old masters counted it.); the grains’ tempers as tells (V.6 keeps them as griefs, with no names); the Standard-Bearer and the standard passing (one hearth-saying stays: choose which heart ye leave them); the seed, the husks, the bladder and the crown-hulls; the root-men’s kinds and their knots of four; the groans and the third cry; the carving names beyond the Book of Knowings; the rules of knowing; and the runners’ law as the in-world form of a Knowing is always true.
  • The world-rules (§6): why a haven need not be a harbour (the Book keeps only Ulden’s line); the double meaning of Rivenkeep; the Ael’thar linguistics beyond ael, now including ael’s meaning; the soft carved form Aelthar; the Orrowen dual and the lintel in the course-hand.
  • Wood names heard no more in the Book: Sethvaren and Saenvael (v2.0); Aelralen, the bond of roots, and four Tide-names, Leathae, Vaelthae, Rhenthae and Eirthae (v3). V.6 keeps Ralenthae (the tide before us) and Aelthae (ours), and V.3 glosses the first once.
  • The soldiers’ names for the Thrones and the wall’s names for the grains (v3): the Leviathan to the Crystal Lich, and Heartoak to Silverbark. They live in §6 and in the game’s battle messages. The Book keeps only The wall named them Mystarchs, and the soldiers the Thrones.
  • The gift’s road by name (IV.4): the warden’s step; Brenn’s pack, a day and a night; the archive chest of Eldhythe under Wendhessa’s seal; the Fall; the Road; the Keep. The Book gives it in the wood’s senses alone, and v3 drops the line that named the arms.
  • The cradle-names of Aldwena, Idrenna and Wendhessa.
  • The Throne table for the VI.3 token (§5.13).
  • Every Translation, unlocked by line (§5.6, §5.7), and every marker’s meaning (§5.13).
  • The map from old tale numbers to new (§2.3), and the clock of the events (§3.5), which the datelines no longer carry.
  • The writers’ notes, word counts and critics’ findings, and the editors’ logs, which §1.12 summarizes.

3The Reckoning and the cast

3.1 · The dating system

  • One hinge: the banner. The Rivenmen count from the dusk the Captain lifted the torn cloak, in deep winter, with snow on the hearth. These notes write it B0, and years before it B−75 and so on. The Book never uses a numeral, and its datelines now say only when a tale was laid (§2.2, J3). The dates of the events are here.
  • Before the banner the hearth counts in generations of the shore, about twenty-five years each (Two generations the Trade Council sent out the axes), and in named ages (§3.2).
  • Inside the siege it counts by winters, W1 to W6. Each siege year runs from its winter to the next, so the second spring is the spring after the second winter.
  • A Tide each winter. Each growing after the first went out as tides go, at the leaf-fall, when its ring was laid, and came out of the breath with the winter. (V.6). The older the wood, the later it came ready, so the Tide and the winter always agree, and either one dates a tale.
  • After the siege the count is the years after the homecomings.
  • The wood counts in summers and rings and never knows the banner. Seren’s headnotes give only the season she knew it in.
  • The Mystaeri live two and a half of our lives (we live twice as long as ye, and half again, II.2), so one of their lives is about three of ours (We listened three of their lives, V.4).

3.3 · The master timeline

“prop.” marks a date the Book never states and these notes propose, so the pieces fit. Everything else is said or implied in the Book.

The elder days

WhenWhat
before all reckoningone people, one tongue, and the parting: untold
ages before the bannerthe Mystaeri are old in the world (Ere your fathers cut their first stone, we were old): the breath, the wood they guide, the pillars at the edge (II.2)
the First Days (prop. before B−375)the first builders come up onto the riven ridge, cleft from its crown to its root, and make a hold of its own stone; the first quarry-warden cuts his name in the lintel of the western stair and left the stone below it for his sons; Aldwena lay the capstone of the inner gate and cut their mark and vow beneath it, where no eye will see it again till the wall fall; the youngest apprentice writes the leaf of the hearth; before they go down, the founders lay the Keep’s slates and the first staff in the black chest, as masons lay by their tools at dusk
the Days of the Havens (from about B−300)Eldhythe first, that men call the Eldest; then the other nine along the coast, Glasspire last, raised over seven generations; each haven’s roll begins This day was the first course laid, and a staff cut for the work.; master-pairs strike coin under their marks; Every wall of the ten was Stonewright work
generation by generationthe Keep goes out of mind, till only the songs held it; no pair comes up to point its mortar; In some winter that none told, the capstone of the inner gate came down from its two posts. / No one heard it fall.

The Harvest (B−120 to B−70)

WhenWhat
B−120the high summer of the havens’ pride; the ships find the black pillars at the hem of the grey; the ship-masters name them the Mystholders; the Trade Council sends out the axes
B−120 to B−70two generations of felling, cargo by cargo: buttresses and bridges, the three spans at Tidesmeet, the pilings of Fenholm, the roof-trees of Holtward, the cranes of Highreach, the galleries of Sandreach, the hall on the ice at Rimewatch, the forges of Emberhythe; the axes beaten out at Carnhold; then a lord’s dancing-hall at Eldhythe, the harbour-houses’ trade-tables and dice-boards, and a merchant’s bed at Tidesmeet
through itthe stumps put up young pillars, and the axemen cut the regrowth again and again for poles, and the guild bought them for scaffold
prop. B−110 to B−90the Mystaeri cut their plea on the mute thing, standing stones at the feet of the pillars yet standing, with Shoreland words among their letters by sound; the axemen carry the stones away with the timber; some hang in the harbour-houses, reduced to ornament, and the guild locks the rest in its archives with its own warning
prop. about B−95three such stones go up over a harbour-house fire on the quay at Eldhythe, where they hang Three generations
the middle yearsthe Stonewrights’ warning is read once in the Council hall at Eldhythe and laid on a shelf
about B−85a clerk’s line under a count of poles: All along the edge, the stumps put up nothing. The grey thins, and the Council names it fortune
B−85 to B−76over the edge the between-light goes hard and white; the newborns of the long-lived come frailer every spring; the elders counsel the trees’ patience (V.4)
prop. B−76one of the long-lived takes the name Aelrhen with his errand, with three words gathered across a long life, and the gift

The Summer of the Guest (B−75, late summer)

WhenAt the outpost and on the shoreOn the water and in the grey
day 1, morningout of the grey came a small grey boat, with no wind; the stranger walks to the warden’s door, and ▒▒▒▒ stood within, and came no further; he speaks in his own tongue, then “Stop… Sky… Dying…”; he kneels and sets down the gift as ye would set down bread before a guest; a gull walks among the stones; ▒▒▒▒ kicked it aside.; his men laugh, and Brenn stands among them; the neck held, the foreheads; the blade touched to the warden’s arm; he tears away, Never in his life had he bled, and the cut goes deep; “Seize him.”; the Guest kneels again, the blade turned to his own arm; “Kill him.”; staves, stones and boat-poles, one of them Brenn’s pole in another man’s hands; both arms broken; drawn by the heels down the shingle; cast into the boat as a fisher casteth a torn net into the bilge; ▒▒▒▒’s order, Leave naught to show; the wood screams; the boat shoved off with the same poles; Tobe washes the poles; Well out, the boat turned. The same day the clerk writes the letter—
day 1, evening—on the road home, the burning Aelvaren rams two timber-boats of the felling, low with black wood; Of the men in them naught is held in the grain; three of the two crews come home on a spar, Daveth among them
day 2, before dawnOn the morrow, ere any woke, Brenn picks the gift out of the dust; at his door ▒▒▒▒ gives him the letter; the gift and the lie go into one pack—
days 2 and 3Brenn runs a day and a night, saying the oath every mile; at Eldhythe the old clerk: “Is it so?” / “It is written.”the Aelvaren runs the hidden channels under the thinnest breath, burning down to a keel and a few ribs
the night it comes home—under the silver elders the council lays its hands on the keel and knows all but why the host drew back; They saw only an act of war.; the keel is laid among the roots, And the roots drank; the young put the question over it; the vote is carried by the weight of anger and grief; that night the first carving, Grow until the shore is silent., is cut into the straightest young tree
that autumnthe three from the spar tell it in every harbour-house and never set foot in a boat again; the shore laughs at them; Brenn, in Eldhythe, laughs not and says naught—
later that yearthe outpost’s men swear in their cups to the turning and the scream, and to three words no two of them agree on—
B−75 to B−70After the year of the burning boat no crew would row within sight of the grey; the near pillars grow scarce and the northern hills send cheaper timber; So the axes stopped. No one decided it.In those same summers the iron came no more to the edge. The grove had not waited to see it.

The vote and the growings (B−75 to the sixth winter)

WhenWhat
after the votethe council chooses its wood: As many years as a wood had when it was chosen, so much could it hold, and the older it was, the later it came ready; the eldest pillars are left standing
B−74the first growing, green wood of one season, is ready, one sign to a hull. At the edge of your sea it lay, while your children had children. Your boats passed it in the breath, and saw naught.
B−75 to about B−3the elders who pleaded go down into the moss one by one; While one elder that had pleaded yet lived, the growing lay ready at the edge of your sea, and went not. (V.4)
about B−3 (prop.)the last of them goes down, and those who cried for war cut the growing loose
through the decadesolder wood is sung into hulls; the carvers are fewer at every growing; those who cried for war died ere their years, under the thin sky, and their own forgat them
W1 to W6each later growing comes ready in its turn and goes out with its winter
the end of W6the last carvers draw the eldest pillars out of the earth and grow them into thrones; Over the last groves the sky came open, hard and white, and the children of the long-lived lie down in the light

The Fall and the Road (B−2 to B0): twenty months

The havens fall in the order they were raised, which is the order III.1 and III.2 tell them.

—the roadbefore the Fall reached him—Kael, of no haven, for the Captain found me on a road

On the Road, when Wendhessa’s arms failed, the masons, who said the archive chest held naught of worth, gave it to the youngest, Tarnel and Seren, a handle each. After the night of the pass Seren carried it alone.

The year of the homecomings, and the years after

WhenWhat
the end of the sixth winterthe ten Thrones come out of the grey as the last fleets go home, and sit down in the havens like herons in the shallows; The wall named them Mystarchs, and the soldiers the Thrones.; Kael lays Count VI; four nights before the going-down Old Ebba dies in her sleep at the fire

the sixth spring to the sixth autumnthe havens retaken one at a time, in the order the Captain chooses (the player’s order); after each fall one of Halyna goes among the timbers and learns the Throne’s heart and name; each haven’s True Man lays its homecoming at its own fire; Hesk dies at Rimewatch, and that night a fisher-girl lights the north lamp; ere their chisels went into the grey, Halyna cut Halvard’s name in the lintel of the western stair
through those monthsinside the heart of the Throne that will fall last, the green sliver grows in the dark: one season’s wood, so it holds one word
the sixth autumnthe tenth Throne falls; the sliver is known: “Home.”, We remember our home too., and Naelear
the days afteron the morrow the boatwrights come down to the shingle; Tarnard lays the keel; Harl bears the first plank to his feet; the stem keyed by Orvenna, Penna holding the last key and Ormund striking it; Halyna’s mark cut across the join by Seren’s lamp; the bow lashing tied with Tarnel’s knot; A day it rode off the point in a hard swell, and took no water.; at the evening tide the sending and the kneeling; VI.3 laid that night
the seventh winterHalyna die in one night, Rhyna first in her sleep and Halvard ere the lamp was out; they lie under the new capstone; the last strip goes over the gate; the Captain goes into the winter in a plain grey cloak
the seventh springon a morning of thin rain the grey boat comes back with the Stone out of the Grey; Ere that spring was out the staff is gone from the gatehouse; from then the inner gate is left open every night
the fourth year after the homecomingsSeren writes the Epilogue (she is about twenty-one); that winter a child asks what the three grey stones say, and she reads the three words on the lowest and lays the Last Note, last of all the leaves
Optional: where the line of keeps stood

I.5 says the Rivenmen raised keeps in this war, like posts along a quay, a crimson pennant on every one. If the game wants an order, the theaters’ debut order gives one and changes no theater: the Keep’s own shore above Eldhythe (Cam 1); the river-land of Tidesmeet (Cam 3); the fen of Fenholm (Cam 5); the woods of Holtward (Cam 7); the cairn-mountain of Carnhold (Cam 9); the burning shore of Emberhythe (Cam 11); the canyon of Sandreach (Cam 13); the frozen mere of Rimewatch (Cam 15, in winter); the crags of Highreach (Cam 17); the cellars of Glasspire (Cam 19). The havens themselves stay held by the grey sails until the Thrones come.

Where the Reckoning was overtaken

The Reckoning study (wf9/reckoning.md) came before the v2 outline, and the Books settled these differently: I.1 is laid on night fifteen, not fourteen; Halyna do not read Brenn in the first winter, for the bundle waits under Wendhessa’s seal until the third (V.1’s three words come from the quay’s old tale); the rolls came in the archive chest, not in the masters’ packs; the archive chest went to Seren and Tarnel on the Road, not to Seren alone; the homecomings fill one year; the Glasspire glass is not given as a cause of the sending. And in v3: the burning has the warden’s reason, to hide the deed, as v1.3 had it, and not v2.0’s custom of the coast.

3.7 · The cast

The laws of the cast
  1. A want, a habit, a line. Everyone named has all three. The want is what a death cuts off or a homecoming pays. The habit is what the listener sees each time the person comes in. The line is what another mouth keeps after the death.
  2. Deaths are fixed points in the war. None depends on how the game is played (§5.4).
  3. Halyna discover, Seren writes, and other hands do the work (§3.9).
  4. The Captain is never named, never tells a tale, and lives. He sits at the end of the bench with his hood up; He will tell you it was not like that. He puts his hood back twice in the whole Book: after IV.3, and in the Epilogue.
  5. No one sails in the grey fleet. The ship is the commander. No one saileth in us.
  6. The warden’s name is always ▒▒▒▒ in stone. In wood he never had one.
  7. No singular they for anyone, and every pair-name takes a plural verb.

3.8 · The deaths

Every death is planted in at least two earlier places in play order, told whole in one place, answered by the hearth, and kept in someone else’s mouth afterward. None is told before the listener has met what caused it.

The rites of mourning

The same every time so the listener learns them:

  1. The east cairns. The ground on the east face will not take a spade, so the Keep’s dead lie under cairns there.
  2. The Bonded lie under their work, their mark beneath the stone, face down, and the wall standing on it: Enrella under the north stone, set by Orvenna’s own four hands; Halyna under the new capstone of the inner gate.
  3. The strip. Told whole once, at Jory: That night the Captain cut a hand’s breadth from his half of the cloak, and bound it on a pike over his cairn. After that, a clause. The crimson on the cairns answers the crimson on the keeps.
  4. The hearth answers. The hood stays up, except after IV.3 and in the Epilogue.
  5. Idrenna’s prayer for the dead, in two halves (V.7 Part I; told without its words at Ebba’s grave).
  6. A kindness remembered, at the cairn or the hearth: one line of the good of the dead, not only the death (§1.9).
  7. Kael names every name at the last count: The rest of the east cairns I named at dusk among the stones, every name, twice.
The strips, which are the art hook’s count

Jory (the east cairns), Corlen (the east cairns), Wick (the east cairns), Enrella (the north stone), Pellow (the east cairns), Ebba (the east face), Halyna (the inner gate), and Hesk (the mere), whose strip the Book no longer tells (§7.2 call 3). At Count VI, after the first five, His half of the cloak hung to his knees the first winter. / Tonight it cannot reach his belt. The five are named there, with the good of each, and For each of them the Captain hath cut a hand’s breadth, as poor men cut the last loaf, their own share thinnest. The deaths told from before the banner (the Guest, Brenn, Wendhessa, Tarnel) and the troop dead under the east cairns take no strip.

Others the Book mourns without a name

The havens’ dead; the children and the old who died on the night of the pass; the men under the east cairns (Some of us looked not, and they are under the east cairns.); the elders of the long-lived who pleaded and went down into the moss one by one; the children of the long-lived who lay down in the light when the Thrones drew up the last of the sky.

4The puzzle pieces

None of these is explained in the Book. Each sits in two or more tales and waits for the listener to see it. “→” means paid off in; “←” means seeded in.

4.1 · The motif that tests every lock

A half waiting for its other half

The bond makes two into one. The Guest’s rite was left half made. The cloak was torn down the back. The first heart split into two planks. The warden’s arm bore the half of something, cut off in the middle. The Last Carver stopped his Title in the middle of a line. Halyna is one name made of two, and their lines come in halves. Every facing spread pairs a stone tale with a wood tale: “A tale told from one side of a wall,” / “is half a tale.” And at the kneeling: “So far can one people go alone: the gift, and the kneeling.” / “The rest needeth the other.” (VI.3). A piece is locked when its other half turns up somewhere else in the Book.

4.2 · The great locks, seed to payoff

The tale-stone and the bloom

The old capstone of the inner gate fell in some winter that none told, with its vow to the earth (the foreword, II.1). Halyna turn it at dusk on the day of the banner, a stone that two must turn, and find their own lines in it: Aldwena’s mark (Rhyna’s forebears) and the wardens’ chisel-bite (Halvard’s fathers), with the bloom grown as pale as frost on a gatepost on its upper face (II.1). The bloom becomes the second oil (I.4). The stone becomes the tale-stone in its niche, under every teller’s hands, Turn it I cannot, for one alone cannot (II.1).

The Stonwryt, each pair’s sacred mark

MarkSeenPaid
Aldwena’sthe foreword (on the tale-stone, drawn, in the Hal) · II.1 (Under it we cut our mark, and our vow beside it)I.4: the same mark on the lid of the black chest: It was the mark of Aldwena. The cradle-rhyme of Aldwena’s line (the first two keep what the first two know) is why Rhyna wist where to look
Halyna’sII.3: drawn once in the hearth-ash for the children, then swept away (Ash is for the wind. A mark is for stone, and stone will hold.)I.4 (cut under the new capstone, unseen: Their mark is not mine to draw.) · VI.3: cut across the join of the stem, Never ere then had a mark of ours gone into wood. / The wood took it. · the Epilogue: on the boat come home, and face down above their grave
Enrella’sII.3 (to be cut under the first stone of their house of one door, for they could not wait for the last) · V.7 Part I (Their mark lay under its first stone. Wendhessa’sthe foreword · V.1 (a bundle under the seal of Wendhessa that no pair would break)IV.3: the seal on Brenn’s bundle, drawn as it was broken: a mark that marks a breaking

The black chest

It holds the Rite on leaves of slate (its first line names itself; lower, the law of the staff); the slates of building for battle (Walls sloped to turn a shot. Gates set crooked against the ram. Courses laid that a breach should fall outward.); the founders’ annal in Aldwena’s we two; the first staff along its floor; three stone flasks, dry; and at the bottom, one leaf more, the youngest apprentice’s, in letters large and careful, as a child’s are, and as mine are yet (I.4).

  • The founders laid it in order when they went down, as masons lay by their tools at dusk. So the Keep was not stripped and abandoned: it was set in order and left, then forgotten around its own chest, the second meaning of It had never fallen. It had only been left. (II.1).
  • The first builders had known war. Against what, the slates say not. Never answered, and never tied to the parting (J25).
  • The battle-slates pay off in Count I: A breach on the south face fell outward, as the slates of the black chest said, and all behind it lived.
  • The apprentice’s leaf pays the foreword’s hearth (At this hearth the first builders of the Keep laid their tales.): So this hearth is older than the havens. We made it not. We forgat it, and found it again at the bottom of a chest.
  • The same vault later holds the enemy’s words, the planks (V.2: the oldest room under this hall, where the Rite was found).

The oils (J9; §2.2)

Cedar answers the felling by not felling (A living tree would they not fell.). The bloom is the forgetting turned to strength. The myrrh of the road’s dead ties the anointing to Tarnel and Wendhessa: I wist for whom it had been carried.

The staff (J10; §2.2)

Seed: the law, read off the slate in halves (I.4). Middle: the ten havens’ staffs left in their falling walls (III.2: A staff is cut for its work, and leaveth not its work.). The Captain leans the first in the gatehouse corner, and stands at the Naming and the staff in its corner (I.5). Payoff: If you doubt the tale, go and look at the dust on it. (I.4), turned in the Epilogue to the clean stroke where it stood, If you doubt the tale, go and look at the dust. The work is done. I know the stair that goes no more., and I have not gone down to look. The cradle-rhyme’s under the stair that goes no more is where the first two kept what they knew, and where the work’s staff is laid down.

The runners and Brenn (J7, J19)

Every haven had its runners’ stone. Hale keeps the law; Brenn broke it. The oath is sworn in I.3, by Brenn at fifteen with mine hand flat on the runners’ stone, and again after IV.3 with Not as Brenn carried. The Guest’s three words were carried by no one; Wick’s three words were carried, and His word came to the Captain, as he had wanted all the war. Brenn says the oath over every mile and I ran it, as Hale does, and the Book never points it out.

The torn cloak and the half-cloak

  • The Title is written in coal from this hearth on the torn cloak; the written half is the banner, the other half the Captain puts back on (I.1). We remember comes out of the Title’s first words.
  • The strips (§3.8): from the first (It was the first strip. / It was not the last.) to the last (It was the last of his half.), and then the plain grey cloak.
  • The Title is cut in stone twice: once by Halyna, hidden under the new capstone in the Captain’s own strokes (It was the Captain’s hand, large and slow. / Neither of them had mended one stroke.); once by the Last Carver, sent across the sea and stopped halfway.
  • The Stone’s grain names it from the other side: He tore his sail, the red one, and set it on the stone.

The archive chest, the sealed bundle, the rolls and the letter

  • One name: the archive chest, the guild’s plain chest out of Eldhythe, never the black chest. Wendhessa bore it out at the Fall, his hand on one handle, hers on the other; on the Road their arms failed, and The masons said it held naught of worth, and gave it to the youngest, Tarnel and me. After the night of the pass she carried it alone. The foreword: Up the mountain I carried two things, our tongue and a chest; and only the chest was heavy.
  • What is in it, and when each comes out:
In the chestFoundTale
the rolls of the havens: their raising, their staffsthe first autumnIII.1 (Halyna had found the rolls in the chest I carried.)
the Harvest rolls: the cargo count, the clerk’s line, the warning, the stained linesread through the first year, to the bottom of the chest on a night of thawIV.1
the letter, copied by a clerk between a count of poles and a count of oars, the name cut out ▒▒▒▒the same nights, read and copied and not understood (What I held, I wist not.)IV.1, then IV.5 (who will argue with writing?), then IV.3

inside it, Brenn’s words wrapped round the giftthe third winter: No pair would break that seal till the wood showed Halyna what the mute stones beside it were. Then Idrenna gave leave.IV.3, then IV.4, then II.2
  • Who cut the name. The guild buried him under his name. The warden’s name it cut away. IV.1 shows the blot, and Rhyna lays her finger on it and takes it away; IV.3 says why.
  • The letter swallowed the rumour. When the three from the spar swore to a burning boat, the Council already had it in writing that a hostile vessel had been burned and released, and the shore laughed: I laughed not, for I had seen it turn. Yet I said naught. The Council had it in writing. (IV.3) · And who will argue with writing? (IV.5). And in the annal, coldly: The Council kept the letter.
  • At the end the chest holds the Book, among the mute stones, by the Guest’s gift and the Stone out of the Grey (the Epilogue).

The burning, and the fear of grey boats (K8; §1.8)

The burning is the warden’s order, to hide the deed: “Burn it,” said he, “and shove it off, and give it the tide. Leave naught to show.” The children’s rhyme keeps his words without knowing it. The irony is never said: the burning boat carried the whole deed home in its boards, so the cover-up is what told the council (IV.6: Wood can hide naught.). The coast’s fear of grey boats comes only from the men who saw it: three from a spar, who told it all that autumn and never set foot in a boat again, and the outpost’s men in their cups. It runs through the Book as plain fear:

TaleThe fear
I.3Jory whistles a tune that the old ship-masters hate, and Merrick bids him stop
IV.1After the year of the burning boat no crew would row within sight of the grey, and the rolls say not why.
IV.5the three ship-masters’ sayings; Let the shore laugh, for the shore will never have to meet it.; I have seen one leave his cup and go out into the rain sooner than hear it.
V.1The old sailors on the wall would not go nigh it.
VI.3Six winters had they turned their faces from the sea when a hull burned. / They did not look away.
the EpilogueThe old ship-masters stood at the head of the shingle, and came no further.; the children go down; Merrick laid his hand on its gunwale.

The ramming, the rumour, and the end of the axes (J21, J22)

The cruellest piece in the Book, which Seren never remarks on: the Guest came to ask that the felling stop; his burning boat’s fury helped stop it; and in the same summers his people voted for war. Seeds: IV.1 (lost at the edge; no crew would row within sight of the grey), IV.5 (two boats; twenty), IV.6 (twice), V.4 (In those same summers the iron came no more to the edge. The grove had not waited to see it.). The rhyme’s two unsung lines have their first dead: and the one it comes for will not come home again; and Merrick, who took the rope, is Daveth’s son.

The mute stones, and the three over the fire

  • The wood asks and the shore answers. IV.2: With the timber the shore-men took the stones away, and whither they went we wist not. IV.1: Some hung in the harbour-houses, reduced to ornament. Men drank under them, and did their reckoning. The rest the guild locked in its archives (It was we who turned the key.). IV.5: three stones over Elspet’s fire, with letters fine as the tracks of birds on wet sand, and The ship-masters knocked out their pipes on the lowest one.
  • They come home: at Eldhythe Voss finds them yet in the wall over a cold hearth, and the lowest scarred where the ship-masters knocked their pipes, and carries them up (VI.1: Long enough have they hung over a dead fire.). In the Last Note they hang over the Long Hearth: Here no pipe is knocked on them.
  • The three words (the Last Note): on the lowest stone, the sounds close and the symbols wrong, Seren finds three words of ours cut by their sound, Stop… Sky… Dying…, cut at the pillars’ feet before the Guest came to say them at the door. She reads them by eye, a stone skill and hers alone; three words of ours I had from Brenn; and she says I think. It is the last thing she learns.

The planks, and the two halves of one heart

  • Seed (V.1): One long edge was raw, split down the grain, as if the rest had been torn away.
  • V.2: the silent plank gave naught, for half a heart holds half a knowing; held at the two ends of the vault at once, the two halves wake every order. At full light Seren lays them raw edge to raw edge: The broken grain met. / It met as the halves of a split log meet. I have not asked Halyna what it meaneth, and they have not told me.
  • VI.3: the planks of the vault become the sending boat, and the two halves, keyed by Orvenna, are its stem: The broken grain met, and held.
  • The Epilogue: By its stem I knew the boat: the first heart keyed, and Halyna’s mark across the join.
  • The Emberhythe plank, Burn, stays out of the boat: the mason lays it on the black crust at Emberhythe’s homecoming, and Harl did not burn it (VI.1; the Book of Knowings).

The green sliver

The first growth any pillar has put up since the iron came: a shoot of the eldest wood, grown in the dark of the last Throne’s heart once its war was done, as seedlings live in the crack of a wall. It is one season’s wood, so it holds one word, as the Song says. Three leaves promise it, each in its own place: No growth had we put up since the iron. We were waiting for the sky to come again. (VI.2) · The iron will tire; and when it stoppeth, the wood will come again. (IV.2) · Wait with them, and when the iron stoppeth, they will grow the sky back. (V.4). VI.3 no longer quotes them. Seren says it in her own words, and lets the reader join them: Since the iron came, not a leaf had the pillars put up. They had waited for the sky to come again. / So it was seed. The sending carries the seed of their sky home: the turn of grace, earned by tales already read.

The sending boat

Built of the vault’s planks: Every one had spoken to us once, and now they would speak for us. Tarnard lays the keel himself, and over the first plank, which Harl bore out of the vault and set at his feet, he says “Wood that knew the sea knoweth the way home.” (IV.6’s own four words). Orvenna key the stem; Halyna cut their mark across the join by Seren’s lamp, and it burned down a finger’s width; Seren ties the bow lashing with Tarnel’s knot, a knot to a mile, and Tarnard says his son’s saying. It carries the sliver on its moss, a Stonwryt coin (It was our dearest gift., answering IV.4’s the dearest is a sliver of such wood), and Halyna’s two chisels, which in the bow … took the last of the light, as in the courtyard (I.2). The whole hearth kneels; Harl walks the bow into the tide; the fisher-children push it off; Well out on the ebb, the boat turned. Into the wind it went, and against the current, and into the grey.: IV.3’s sentence without burning.

The Stone out of the Grey, and the boat that came back

In the seventh spring the same boat comes in, known by its stem and Halyna’s mark. In its bow, where the sliver and the chisels lay, is Myststone, stone that had been wood, like the Guest’s gift, which the Rivenmen … say … remembereth all. Seren knows the chisel’s bite: Twice had I held the lamp for that chisel, under the new capstone and across this stem. The Last Carver learned our letters (Our cloak he never saw, for he never sailed. Our letters he learned.) and cut In the memory of our home, our families..., with one word our Title never had, the, before memory. I read it as it was cut, and no child laughed. The cutting stops mid-line, and its last stroke is sharp yet. His fate stays untold. We have not yet earned a guest. · What we will send back, I know not. I know only that it must be sent kneeling.

The answer, from first to last

Ebba says We remember first, the first night the hearth asked a tale (the foreword); she begins it a breath early most nights, and after IV.3 Old Ebba began it, and the rest were slow to follow; she dies, and the hearth waits a breath for her (I.5; Fenholm’s fire, VI.1); after VI.3 the hearth waits the width of one old voice, and her great-granddaughter, the fisher-girl who asked whither the green wood would go and went foremost into the sea, says it to the wood (VI.3); in the Epilogue the first child to lay her hands on the boat that came back is the same child, and she keeps the north light.

4.5 · The fuses that must burn in play order

All of these run seed before payoff in the play order of §5.3.

  • The letter: IV.1 (Cam 10) → IV.5 (Cam 12) → IV.3 (Cam 15).
  • The rumour before the truth: IV.5 (Cam 12) → IV.3 (Cam 15) → IV.4 (Cam 18) → IV.6 (Cam 20).
  • The bundle opened: the foreword says at first launch that the seal was broken once the wood showed Halyna what the mute stones were, so the delay is foretold; IV.2’s reading (from Cam 11; fallback Cam 14) shows the mute thing’s plea before IV.3 (Cam 15).
  • The halves: V.1 (Cam 1) → V.2 (Cam 13) → VI.3 (the Finale) → the Epilogue.
  • The marks: the foreword (first launch) → I.4 (Cam 2) → II.1 (Cam 3) → II.3 (Cam 4, Halyna’s) → V.7 I (Cam 25, Enrella’s) → VI.3 → the Epilogue. Aldwena’s mark is seen twice before it is explained, which is fine for lore; after, never before binds fleet behaviours, not lore.
  • The growth: VI.2 (each Throne), IV.2 (Cam 11 and 16) and V.4 (Cam 23) → VI.3.
  • The deaths: every death’s plants (§3.8) come before the leaf that tells it. Two plants were checked against play order. Jory is alive in V.1 (Cam 1) and I.3 (from Cam 2), dies in Cam 2, and is told in Count I (Cam 6), IV.5 (Cam 12) and I.5 (Cam 32). The foreword’s dead teller that held up a wet finger to every wind is read at first launch, but no player can know whose finger it is until III.1 (Cam 5) teaches Corlen’s habit; between Cam 5 and his death at Cam 9 a player who rereads the foreword can guess (§7.2 call 17).
  • Corlen’s last tale: III.2 (Cam 8) → his death (Cam 9) → Count II (Cam 14).
  • The answer’s heir: the foreword and Ebba’s habit (first launch onward) → I.5 (Cam 32) → VI.3 (all ten fallen) → the Epilogue.
  • The Mystarchs: Count VI (Cam 32, last battle) names the Thrones Mystarchs before I.5 (Cam 32 complete) has Ebba say the last Mystarch.

What changed in v3

Seren no longer speaks the seams. v2.0’s lines (The wood says two. The ship-masters say twenty. I have kept both. and its kind) are cut, for they decoded the facing leaf (craft rule 10; voice §4). The foreword’s saying, Our rolls forget, and can be made to lie. The wood cannot lie, but it cannot see fear., stands once above all of them, and the Invocation says Where the two agree not, we have kept both every night. Every stone leaf of a spread is named facing its wood leaf in the Contents; no headnote names it.

5How the Legends enter the game

For the integrator. Nothing in this section touches the simulation: every surface named here is cosmetic and sits off the sim path. Where it names a design document, it says “the fleet design (admiral doc)”; there is no admiral in the fiction. The laws and the schedule are v2.0’s; what v3 changed is marked.

5.1 · The standing laws

The standing laws

Laws 1 to 7 are v1.3’s, laws 8 to 12 v2.0’s. Law 12 is restated for v3, and law 13 is new.

  1. Cosmetic, off the sim path. No leaf, Whisper or message changes a number, a spawn or a behaviour. Every unlock is keyed to a campaign milestone or to an event the sim has already recorded, so two players who play exactly the same way open exactly the same leaves at exactly the same moments.
  2. Low-load moments only. Lore appears at first launch, in the debrief, in the interstitial between campaigns, and at battle load. It never appears during timed Build or a live Fight. Two fleet surfaces are not lore and follow the fleet design instead: the between-sortie dispatch (§5.12), and, from the Tide that Learned Deceit, the root sign a sunk hull of the running carving floats for the rest of its sortie (a drawn glyph with no words; admiral doc §9b). The root sign is Seren’s eye-work: Their shapes I learned by eye, at the tide-line and in the vault, and only the rest of a carving must be known.
  3. After, never before. A tale about a fleet behaviour unlocks only after the player has met that behaviour. The Knowing’s law (it explains this battle and never hints at the next) binds the tales too.
  4. No forced playstyle. Every unlock is reachable by any way of holding a battle. Sinking a fleet and routing it both yield wood. No leaf is earned only by one particular counter.
  5. Every EVENT unlock has a CAMPAIGN fallback, so no player misses a leaf.
  6. One new leaf per debrief or interstitial. Any others queue for the next. A ripening (a reading’s story filling in beneath it, a line Added in Seren’s hand appearing, a native mark translated) shows as a small mark and does not count toward the one.
  7. The Book only adds. It never removes or changes a word the player has already read.
  8. Deaths are fixed points. Each death is keyed to a campaign milestone (or, for Hesk, to Rimewatch being taken back), never to how a battle went (§5.4).
  9. A dead voice falls silent. From the campaign of a death, no battle message or Whisper is spoken in that voice. The dead person’s line passes to the mouth that keeps it (§3.8). The raised men’s True Man lines begin at their raising.
  10. A death is told first by its leaf. The one exception is a single hearth-word at the end of the death’s own campaign (its interstitial), which follows the fact and tells no more than the fact (§5.4).
  11. The lore is true, as the Knowing is. The runners’ law, “What I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else.”, is the in-world form of the rule that a Knowing is always true. A dispatch is what is seen, as a runner carries it.
  12. The Book is the canon. Plain Words and Original carry every beat of the Book, in order, and every marker and native block. v3: they follow it beat for beat and anchor for anchor, not paragraph for paragraph, for Plain Words is a retelling with its own paragraphs (§1.10, §5.8). Where they differ in a fact, the Book is right.
  13. One switch (K1, new). The reader’s tab is one choice for the whole Book. Switching it at any leaf switches every leaf, and the reader keeps its place by the shared anchors (§5.8).

5.2 · The prologue (first launch)

  • What plays. The Invocation, then the climb-and-writing passage of I.1, marked PROLOGUE EXCERPT: from The True Men looked to their Captain. to That was the last unbroken wall of the Shorelands, and that was the banner over it. It plays on the torn-cloak art, is skippable, plays once, and can always be read again in the Book.
  • Its length, v3. The excerpt is about 300 words in ten paragraphs with the Title (v2.0’s was about 455; v1.3’s 225), and the Invocation about 200. Read at an easy pace that is about ninety seconds, inside v1.3’s target. Recommended, as before: pace it by beats, one paragraph to a beat of the art (the look at the wall; the climb; the cloak taken off; the silence and the child’s cough; He tore it down the back.; the writing and the binding; the reading; the Title alone; the banner given to the wind), advanced by the player. v2.0’s fallback, a shorter cut for the prologue only, is no longer needed (§7.1).
  • The excerpt stands alone. v3 begins it at The True Men looked to their Captain. so it needs nothing before it. The Plain excerpt (about 250 words) stands alone the same way, and plays under Plain Words.
  • What the Book shows after it. The first spread: I.1 on the left; on the right the wood’s grain, the Stone’s own rings with no mark in them, and beneath them one line in Seren’s course-hand, which the player cannot yet read: Untold. Halyna cannot yet hold this deep. Its translation comes with The Mortar Line at Cam 1 complete (§5.7). The leaf stays bare of the wood until the Epilogue fills it at the very end. From the first minute the player sees the shape of something not yet understood, in both the hands that are to be learnt.
  • The foreword is in the codex from the start (recommended), but the prologue does not show it. It is the key to the frame: the lintel over the pair-names, laid, the answer and its choosing, the two inks and the pieced readings, the silence after the wood, the unbonded scribe, and the tale that is the teller’s in words that are Seren’s. Its dateline says it was written in the third summer, and it says The Book is half full. Its costs, v3: it tells of One of its tellers, that held up a wet finger to every wind, already under the east cairns, which a player can read as Corlen once III.1 (Cam 5) has shown his habit, four campaigns before he dies; it shows Enrella alive, the youngest, in the present tense; and it says the seal was broken till the wood showed Halyna what the mute stones beside it were, which IV.2 and IV.3 later make plain (§7.2 call 17).

5.3 · The tales by campaign (play order)

Story order is not play order. The cycle opens at the wall in the middle of things and reaches back, and the cosmology arrives last, as memory. The play order is the hearth’s own order, the nights on which the tales were laid.

How to read the When column
  • A bare campaign number (“Cam 5”) means the interstitial shown when that campaign is complete.
  • A debrief is the screen after one battle.
  • An event gate (“From Cam 11, the first … met”) fires at the debrief of the first battle in which the sim recorded that event, from the named campaign on. If the event has not happened by its fallback, the leaf opens at the fallback instead.
  • When two leaves fall due on the same surface, the one higher in this table shows, and the other waits for the next surface (law 6).

The campaigns and the seasons (the Reckoning’s calendar, which the datelines follow)

  • Why I.3 opens from Cam 2. I.2’s headnote says this hearth had heard but one before it, V.1 is laid at the first month’s end, and I.3 in its second month. Opening I.3 from Cam 2 keeps the hearth’s order, keeps Jory alive in it (he dies late in Cam 2), and still lands it after the player has used the calls. Fallback: v1.3’s Cam 1 gate (§7.2 call 18).
  • The Homecomings and the Thrones in the Book. An entry is laid in the player’s order but stands in its haven’s place, in the order of raising. An entry not yet earned shows as its haven’s name and nothing more.
  • Breathing room. Campaigns 7, 9, 11, 17, 19, 21, 28 and 30 still have no scheduled leaf, only their Whisper strand and any ripening. (An event leaf may land in Cam 11, since IV.2’s gate opens there; Cam 9 carries Corlen’s death as a hearth-word and a ripening.)
  • The debrief notice. When a leaf unlocks, the debrief shows one line and no more: “A new tale is laid at the hearth.” · “Halyna have told more of the wood.” (with the lintel over the name).
  • The interstitial (changed in v3). It shows the campaign’s leaf from its first line to the end of the first sentence after about 80 words (never past about 120; never mid-sentence; never past a halt or a strike line), with Read on in the Book. v2.0 had widened this to 140 words for its long sentences; v3’s sentences never run past 30 words, so v1.3’s 80 to 120 holds again. The whole leaf is always in the Book, and nothing blocks play.

  • The raised men. Voss speaks as a corporal until Cam 9 and as a True Man from Cam 9 (Corlen’s battery). Ruan speaks as a file-leader until Cam 29 and as a True Man from Cam 29, when the Captain raised him a True Man. Hale refuses the battery and stays a runner. Kael’s roll is fourteen names, and twelve to answer at Count VI and Fourteen names, and eleven to answer after Hesk.
  • No death waits on a battle’s outcome. A lost battle in a death’s campaign changes nothing; the death happens at its milestone. The one keyed to a battle, Hesk’s, is keyed to Rimewatch being taken back, which every player reaches.
  • The hood (changed in v3). The Book keeps the Captain’s hood up, and puts it back twice: after IV.3, the first Amen over a killing by our own, and in the Epilogue. So wherever the game shows the Captain at the hearth (the debrief art, Open the Book), his hood is up, and it is back only on the Cam 15 interstitial that opens IV.3 and at the Epilogue. The deaths are shown by the half-cloak, shorter by a strip at each (§5.12). v2.0 put the hood back on every death’s debrief.

5.5 · The Knowing: the wood leaves, unlocked by depth

The rule of the wood

Mystwood is not read. The wood is not read but known, whole, as a memory is known. Touch it and its tale is known whole; the hard part is the telling, So Halyna give it up in pieces, the one taking up where the other’s words fail. Only one of Halyna need touch the wood, for what one knows the other knows. At first a shore-mind receives only the shape of a knowing, for the wood’s thoughts are a forest’s (the rest was a forest’s thought, and they had but the shore’s). Over the Tides Halyna learn to hold more of it and to tell it. Every wood leaf is gated by how much Halyna can hold and tell, and that rises Tide by Tide, by campaign bands, so it is deterministic.

Knowing depthBandWhat Halyna can doWood leaves reachable
one shapethe Hasty Tide (1–6)know that the wood means something, and tell one word of itnone (the Knowing line only)
two shapes, held at oncethe Second Tide (7–14)hold two signs together; tell the Hasty wrecks back whole (V.2)IV.2, the reading only
sentences, and the memory in themthe Tide of Remembering (15–22)tell sentences, and the memory in themIV.2 whole · V.3 · IV.4 · IV.6
tiesthe Joined Tide (23–26)hold who waited for whom, and so many hulls at onceV.4 · V.6
aimthe Tide that Learned Deceit (27–31)tell what a carving wantsII.2 (all but one word)
a whole morrowthe Last Tide (32)hold and tell a whole planV.7 Part III (a stone leaf)
