How a wood leaf looks (changed in v3, §1.5)
  • Blank. The leaf’s own round of grain, fully cut and not glossed, and beneath it one line in Seren’s course-hand: Untold. Halyna cannot yet hold this deep. Only the leaf facing I.1 shows its rings uncut. Every wood leaf shows blank from the moment its stone leaf opens, so the player sees the empty half of the spread.
  • The reading alone. Only IV.2 opens this way, at its shape gate: the dateline, the headnote and Halyna’s reading, with its two marks drawn and not yet told (legend_iv2_gap1, legend_iv2_gap2). The reading is the shape of a knowing before it could be told. v2.0’s As first known, … the shape only fragments are gone; the reading does their work.
  • Whole. The dateline, the headnote, the reading, then the story from This is held in the grain. in three rings parted by <!-- RING -->, and the return, …and the grain holds it still. / And no one answered. The builder keys a wood leaf on <!-- READING --> and on its first story line, This is held in the grain. At Cam 16 IV.2’s story ripens beneath its reading, which stays, for the Book never removes a word.
  • The two inks on a wood leaf. The reading’s lines alternate the two Halyna inks line by line, and neither is named. The story is in the wood role, in one ink. The headnote is stone, in Seren’s ink. v2.0 alternated whole paragraphs of the story in two inks under <!-- HALYNA -->; v3 does not.
  • The name swap. Inside wood leaves the wood names things by its own words: the black pillars are our pillars and the Naelsaen; the mist is the breath; the warden is the host, the master of that house. From VI.2 on, the Thrones speak by the names they gave themselves. The last swap is the wood’s own name, Naelear, which fills the untold word of II.2 when VI.3 is laid.
  • The silence. Every wood leaf closes And no one answered. The hearth never answers the wood until VI.3.
  • Halyna’s two inks on stone leaves. II.3 is theirs whole, inside one HALYNA block, and alone names its tellers (<!-- MARKED -->), for the children. IV.3’s reading of Brenn is theirs, inside another, alternating by paragraph. And wherever Halyna speak a line in halves in another teller’s leaf, the halves stand inside their own block and take the two inks: the foreword (1), I.2 (2), I.4 (3), IV.1 (1), IV.3’s annal (1), V.1 (1), V.5 Count VI (1), V.7 (4) and VI.3 (3). That is nineteen blocks in all. Other pairs’ halves (Idrenna’s, Orvenna’s, Enrella’s) stay in the leaf’s one ink.
  • Stone and wood on the page. The builder sets stone text and wood text in two roles, per paragraph: the wood in a barely different old-style face, the stone ink a shade toward yellow and the wood ink a shade toward green, still one document. Seren’s headnotes are stone even on wood leaves. Wood-role passages inside stone leaves are marked <!-- W -->: V.2’s seven carvings; V.7’s three carvings and Part II’s italic line; VI.3’s We remember our home too. and line 3 of The Two Homes. The Epilogue’s inscription is <!-- MIXED -->.

The Book of Knowings is the fleet’s own half of the Book.

  • Forty-six carvings under ten roots: seven of the young wood, eight of each of the three Tides after it, seven of the Tide that Learned Deceit, and eight Judgements of the Thrones (two Thrones carried an older carving whole). The Last Tide carved no new head, only whole morrows and at the end of each a turning for home. Burn, a sign of the Fall, stands apart: I count it not among these.
  • In the game each entry fills when its carving has been met and survived, by any route. Until then only its root sign shows. An entry gives the carved line, the root sign, Seren’s gloss, a plain gloss of what the ships did, and, once the carving has been known by name, its counters (admiral doc §9d).
  • Fidelity follows depth. An entry is first written at the depth of the band in which its carving was met, and ripens when the depth passes it. The seven Hasty entries hold Seren’s one word each until V.2 is laid at Cam 13, and then bear the soldiers’ names, worn smooth with handling; Second-Tide entries keep their gaps until Cam 15.
  • The Thrones’ column (Whose it was at the last) stays blank until that Throne falls. Leavaren’s line reads the Bearer of New Branches.
  • Burn shows below the table when V.2 is laid. Its clause of the black crust at Emberhythe ripens with Emberhythe’s homecoming.
  • The roots and the growth lines (admiral doc): the Wave is Flood, the Lone Stroke is Sounding, the Bar Before is Screen, the Spark is Hunt, the Mute Square is Siege, the Two Mouths are Feint, the Turned Stern is Lure, the Breath is Bleed, the Knot is Patience, and the Smoothed Cut is Mask.
  • The Last Note is the last leaf of the Book: Seren reads the Guest’s three words on the lowest of the three grey stones, by their sound (§4.2). It opens at the next launch after the Epilogue (§5.3), with its drawing (§5.6).

5.6 · The native hands on unearned leaves

The Book is a translation

Every leaf the player reads in English was first laid in a hand of its own. The Rivenmen’s is the course-hand: their tongue, Orrowen, written in a chisel alphabet of straight strokes that stands on a mortar line, each word a stone; its old form, the first builders’ hand, is the Hal. The wood’s is the grain: it holds meaning and not sound, as a round of growth rings read from the heart to the bark, with its marks cut across the rings. A surface the player has not yet earned stays in its own hand. Earning it adds the translation beneath the hand, and the hand stays (law 7). The tongues and the hands are specified in the Tongues doc.

Every drawing in the Book’s text

The Book carries each as a ::: native block (or a ::: note for a mark set inside a line, written ⟦id⟧) with its in-world caption and its fold. Every key and kind is v2.0’s. The Translation, unlocked by lines live here.

  • In its own hand in the game, beyond the Book’s text: every blank wood leaf (its round, with Seren’s line beneath); each Throne’s name, kept in the bark until that Throne falls or, if the battle is lost, until its haven is retaken (admiral doc, T-rule 9); the Book of Knowings, where an unmet entry shows its root sign alone; the Knowing line in the debrief, where the drawn root sign replaces the admiral doc’s placeholder ᛫; and the root sign a sunk hull floats (law 2).
  • Read at once, in the hand and in English: the Title, in both its cuttings; the rolls, the warning and the warden’s letter, which their tales already quote; and the Last Carver’s letters, cut so that one could read it alone.
  • Never read: Seren’s empty leaf facing V.4 stays empty of every hand, as she asked. The warden’s name is cut out of its stone. The three stained lines of the roll are lost. What was seen in the grey on Glasspire’s night is not in the grain. Halyna’s mark under the new capstone is never drawn there (Their mark is not mine to draw.); the player meets it only in the ash, on the stem and on the boat.
  • Truth or nothing. Every drawing is a true encoding by the Tongues doc’s rules, so a player who decodes it reads what the translation says. Where no native text has been authored, nothing is drawn, and nothing is faked. This binds the Original tab too, until it is set down (§5.8).
  • The accessible titles never leak. A locked drawing’s accessible title says only what it is (A leaf in the course-hand, not yet read; A carving in the grain, not yet known; A pair’s mark), never its English.
  • The marks a player can match: Aldwena’s (the foreword, I.4); Halyna’s (II.3, VI.3, the Epilogue); Enrella’s (V.7 Part I, alone); Wendhessa’s broken seal (IV.3); and the three words on the hearth-stone (the Last Note), against the sounds Brenn gave in IV.3.
  • Still owed by the Tongues team: the four pair-marks; the soft letters of the mute stones, so a decoding player can find the Guest’s three words on the lowest stone; the Rite’s slates in the Hal; the names in the lintel of the western stair, Halvard’s last; the round of each blank wood leaf, composed by rule; the ten small rounds of VI.2; and the Orrowen and grain of every leaf, for the Original (§5.8).

5.7 · The script-unlock ladder

The native hands translate on the rungs of the Tongues doc’s unlock ladder (its §12.2). Each rung is a milestone every player reaches. Each wood rung is one band of the Knowing given a name; each stone rung is a hand the player can now read. A translation added beneath a hand already on a leaf is a ripening, not a new leaf. The names are proposals.

  • The debrief notice for a rung is one line: “A leaf in the course-hand is read.” for a stone rung, and “Halyna have told more of the wood.” for a wood rung.
  • Skill achievements (Pacifist and the rest) may add one gloss line at most, never a translation. Pacifist could add Seren’s soft readings of the signs to the sign-list.
  • The schedule is unchanged. The Hasty entries still ripen at Cam 13, the Second-Tide gaps at Cam 15, and IV.2 comes whole at Cam 16.
  • Every tab. A drawing is the same under The Book, Plain Words and Original; only its translation changes language (the caption and the gloss line may be put in plain words under Plain Words), and Original gives none.

5.8 · The reader: three tabs, one switch

Open the Book shows three tabs at the head of every tale, and of the foreword, the Invocation, the Epilogue and the Book of Knowings:

TabWhat it isState now
The BookSeren’s English: this Book, v3.0.0, the canondone (wf11/book_v3.md)
Plain Wordsthe same Book told again in easy modern English, from its beats: every event, name, number, teller and line kept, in its own sentences and paragraphs; about grade 3; no archaism; contractions where a speaker would use them; Jack’s fixed lines and the liturgy in their Plain forms; the pair-names under their lintel, the blots, the markers and the native blocks exactly as the Book has them; no datelines. v3.1: every paragraph rewritten whole for a reader new to the lore, the lore explained as the tale meets it, a plain when line in place of each dateline, and a note for new readers before the foreworddone (wf13/plain_v31.md, §1.16; v3.0’s was wf11/plain_v3.md, §1.10)
Originalthe Book in its own hands: the stone leaves, headnotes, foreword, Invocation and Epilogue in Orrowen, in Seren’s leaf-hand; the wood leaves in the grain, each round whole and then ring by ringtranslated, merged and on the story page (K2; §1.15, §1.16): set down from v3 by the Tongues team; on the page every line of a Romanisation fold stands word over word, each word over its English (v3.1.0). Being set down anew in its own hands. does not ship (§1.11)
  • One switch (K1). The choice is one for the whole Book: switching at any leaf switches every leaf. It is remembered with the settings, not the save. The first choice is The Book.
  • Keeping the reader’s place. The three texts line up by the anchors they share, in the same number and order (§1.11): the headings, the bold part heads, the nineteen HALYNA blocks, the eight READING blocks, the sixteen RING rules, the native blocks and notes, the W markers, the MARKED, TOKEN, MIXED, RIPENS, PROLOGUE EXCERPT and FACING LEAF markers, every song and every hearth’s answer. On a switch the page lands on the last anchor above the line in view, then the same fraction of the way to the next. Under Original a wood leaf gives one ring to a paragraph.
  • Original translates nothing. It shows the native hands and nothing else, for the player who decodes.
  • A locked leaf, or a locked mark inside a leaf, shows in its own hand under every tab until it is earned. When it is earned, The Book and Plain Words add its translation beneath the hand, each in its own words, and the hand stays. A leaf earned whole is set down in Original whole. No tab shows a leaf or a mark that the Book does not yet hold.
  • The grain’s glosses follow the tab: shore, the mute thing and the dread in the Book; the Plain’s own plain words under Plain Words; Seren’s own Orrowen words under Original. The tabs show on a locked leaf too, so the player learns where its translation will appear.
  • The lintel in the reader. In The Book and Plain Words a pair-name is drawn under a fine overline the width of the name, as a lintel over two posts, wherever the source writes {{Name}}. The stroke is decoration to a screen reader, which reads the name as written. In Original the name stands under a lintel line in the course-hand, and its verb takes the dual.
  • The two inks in the reader follow §5.5: the readings line by line; Halyna’s blocks by paragraph or by half; II.3 by its marked tellers, with its italic lines in Seren’s ink and the opening and the oldest saying in neither Halyna ink.
  • The story page (The Legends) carries the three tabs inside the page, with the one switch, as a working model of the reader. The copy Jack shares (K3) is that whole story page on the web, with every leaf and every native drawing, private to the people he chooses; never a trimmed copy (§1.11).

5.9 · The Whispers and the hearth-lines

Where they live

In the debrief and the interstitial, never in the first Build. Each band has three strands:

  • a hearth-word: a short line drawn from the tales, spoken by Halyna, one of the Twelve, or another voice of the wall;
  • the Knowing: what Halyna knew from the battle’s own wreck-wood, in Seren’s words. The lines below are samples; the live line is generated from the sim’s record of the carving that actually ran, so it is always true, and its fidelity follows knowing depth (§5.5);
  • a wood-word, from Cam 15 on: a line from a wood leaf’s reading, in Halyna’s pieces.
The rules
  • A line tagged ⟨tale⟩ enters its band’s pool only once that tale (or that ripening) has opened. A line that names a fleet behaviour waits until the player has met it. After, never before.
  • A line that quotes a tale quotes it word for word in the v3 Book’s form (hath, must, thee, lasteth). Lines marked (J) are Jack’s GDD Whispers and stay in his words; a line marked (dep) must stay, for a tale quotes it or answers it.
  • Recommended (§7.2 call 19): a hearth-word that quotes a tale follows the reader’s tab, in the Book’s words under The Book and the Plain’s under Plain Words.
  • A Halyna line comes in two halves, a voice each.
  • A dead voice falls silent (law 9). After a death, the dead person’s line may come back only in the mouth that keeps it (§3.8).
  • Faith is never the butt of a joke.
  • We remember. is never a Whisper. Nor is We remember our home too., which lives only in VI.3 and never appears in store art or screenshots.

The Finale · the Thrones · recognition

  • Knowing, one for each Throne: “Here is the name it gave itself, the haven it sat in, and the judgement it was grown to carry.” (filled from its VI.2 entry)
  • The True Man of that haven, or the man who speaks for him (Voss for Eldhythe, Kael for Rimewatch, Ruan for Glasspire), once its Throne has fallen: a line from his VI.1 entry.
  • “Someone must keep a light.” (the hearth-word after Rimewatch, in the fisher-girl’s voice)
  • Dispatch: “It has sat among the bones of its kin in [haven] since it came. Count the groans.”
Dropped or recast in v3

Every quoted line takes the v3 wording (hath, must, thee, lasteth, ye, whoso kneeleth, called home, obeyeth, lieth not, kept no man alive). Brenn’s I laughed. I have not laughed at anything since. is gone with the Book’s line, and his mirror takes its place. We will not lay it all on the Council. goes with the Book’s. The shore laughs at the grey boat. The ship-masters do not. becomes a ship-master’s own saying. Kael’s Never yet did the grey sails tell a lie that did not show its seam goes with Count V’s. It’s only a rhyme moves from Voss over the cairn, which v3 no longer has, to Jory alive. The wood-words are now lines of the readings. The Plain’s forms (Someone has to keep a light., Hold it, and it will hold you.) are for the Plain tab only.

5.10 · Battle messages

The style rules
  1. When, and for how long. At battle load or briefing, or during the 3-2-1 transition, for three seconds. Never during timed Build or a live Fight.
  2. The draw. Each battlefield has a pool of 5 to 8 messages, and a replay shows a different one. The draw is cosmetic and never touches the battle’s seed. Because of rule 7, any draw gives every player the same information.
  3. Who speaks.
    • Wall-voices: Hale, Voss, Kael and the other True Men by name (the Twelve, then Voss and Ruan, §3.7), Wick and the runners, Jory and the soldiers of the six companies, wall-hands, file-leaders, lookouts, masons.
    • Seren and Halyna speak rarely. A Halyna line is two halves, a voice each.
    • The other pairs, in halves: Idrenna, who take the long view; Orvenna, who speak of the wall and never raise their voices; Enrella, the young pair, who finish each other’s jests (while they live).
    • Merrick and the old ship-masters, who fear the sea and the grey boat, and say so plainly. There is no saying of the coast.
    • A Trade Council lord who fled to the Keep, whose old pride is turning.
    • The Captain never speaks in a message. There are no admirals, generals or modern ranks, for there was never a navy and the generals were slain; and no one is aboard the fleet.
  4. The dead fall silent (§5.4): Jory leaves every pool from Cam 2, Corlen from Cam 9, Wick from Cam 16, Enrella from Cam 25, Pellow from Cam 29, and Hesk after the Rimewatch battle. Voss’s True Man lines begin at Cam 9 and Ruan’s at Cam 29.
  5. Length. At most 16 words, in one or two sentences.
  6. Register (changed in v3). Soldiers’ speech of their world, shorter and plainer than the Book. The Book is now in heavy old English; a message is read in three seconds, so it takes only the light end of that: old word order and the stock words (ere, naught, yet, for as a joining word) are welcome, and -eth, thou and the old pasts are not, unless the line quotes a tale. No contractions in new lines; Jack’s own lines keep theirs. No upon. Humour comes from character, never from anachronism. No design vocabulary and no mechanic named. Sortie is allowed in soldiers’ mouths only, and so is treelings. At most one oath in a pool, and faith is never the butt of a joke.
  7. Hint discipline. If one message in a pool hints at the battle’s carving, every message in that pool carries the same hint in a different voice. Otherwise none of them does. A hint may name a tell that fits the Tide. It never names the counter.
  8. Truth. Never say what the fleet will do. What the fleet remembers lasts one siege only, for the wood remembers the wall, not the hand. A Throne line may count groans. The soldiers’ fear-names for the Thrones (§6) are game words, in no leaf of the Book; use them only until that Throne’s true name has been told in its VI.2 entry. A treeling line goes only in the pool of a battlefield that lands root-men, and a seed line only where crown-hulls sail. No horn, ever: the runners reach the Captain, and he calls by his own voice.
  9. Flavour. The Tide sets the fleet’s behaviour: one-word mobs, then pairs, then looking, then three roads, then seams, then morrows, then groans. The theater sets the texture: salt, river, fen, canopy, cairn, ember, sand, rime, crag-wind, crystal in the dark.

Examples

v1.3’s thirteen stand; the v2.0 proposals stand with the v3 changes marked.

Jack’s lines, kept

Word for word unless a note says otherwise (spelling follows the Book’s harbour):

  • “The salt air stings. The walls don’t care.”
  • “Grandfather built this harbour wall. I’ll be damned if I let it fall.” (its pool’s one oath)
  • “Two sails on the horizon, and not keeping station. This will be easy.” (edited in v1.3: “Standard formation” contradicts a Hasty Tide that keeps none)
  • “The Commander says hold the coast. We hold the coast.” (the wall calls him Commander; the hearth calls him the Captain)
  • “Fresh mortar, fresh stones. The wall stands renewed.”
  • “Ice in the mortar. Ice in my bones. The wall still holds.”
  • “Voss asked why we fight for ice and stone. I said it’s not the ice I’m fighting for.” (The speaker is Stannard, who gave that answer in I.5.)
  • “The wind carries their drums. Let it carry our cannons’ answer.” (the drums are the knocking of the wood along the bond of roots)
  • The Finale only, filled from the sim: “Three keeps held against the Leviathan. The Stonewrights will sing of the east wall.” (a fear-name, before the true name is told)
  • The Last Garrison: “The keep is falling! Every hand to the guns!”
Cut, or recast
  • “They sent the Leviathan against the Frozen pass. We sent it back.” A Throne cannot appear outside the Finale, so it becomes “They sent a great hull against the Frozen pass. We sent it back.”
  • “You fled the Sandworm last time, Commander. It remembers.” It implies the fleet remembers across sieges, so in the Finale only it becomes “The Deep-Beneath has sat under Sandreach since it came. Count the groans.” (the wall’s English name, which VI.1 uses).
  • Any line with a horn, a horn-call or a signal flag is cut.
  • Any line that gives the coast a custom of burning, or a saying about what the grey sends, is cut.

5.11 · Command Time in the fiction

  • Jack’s ruling (2026-09-26), unchanged. A day has 24 hours and an hour 60 minutes. At the pace of the siege a day passes in 18 real seconds, so an hour goes by in 0.75 s. The racing hour is meant: it is the urgency of the holding.
  • Under command. When the player starts an order in a Fight, time ramps down (it does not snap) until an hour takes 18 real seconds: 1/24 speed, the hour as long as a day was, the minutes ticking every 0.3 s. When the order is committed or cancelled, time dwells a moment at that floor, so a second planned order can follow without racing the ramp, then ramps back up. Orders are stamped in sim ticks, so wall-clock speed never changes an outcome. The ramp and dwell numbers live in admiral doc §12, which restates the tick-to-hour mapping under the 24-hour day; the Fight doc must still restate any grid-per-second figures.
  • The command, told as fiction. The Captain gives each True Man his mind before the assault: where to watch, what shot to lay, and when to hold (the Deploy order card). In the holding each of the Twelve fights his battery by his own judgement, because he knows that mind; that is why each battery fights on its own (auto-fire running the player’s plan). The Captain calls one battery at a time, with his own voice, standing at its side. The three calls, which the troops drill every dawn (I.3), are the game’s three Fight verbs:
    1. Shift your ground: the water a battery watches, not the stones it stands on (re-task the zone);
    2. Change your shot: for the sea, the shore or the sky (switch domain);
    3. Hold, or loose (fire control).

    Shift your ground, and watch new water;
    change your shot for the sea, the shore, the sky;
    hold, or loose, when the Captain is by.

    The calls are orders to a battery of many men, and are never put into thou.

  • Command Time is the walk. When the Captain goes along the wall to a battery and stands at its side to call it, the hour stretches around him: It stopped not short. It slowed, like a stone-cart on a long hill, till a boy behind might count the spokes., and lets go slow, like a held breath going out (I.3). The stamp is the tick, not the clock.
  • One at a time is slow. The mechanic’s limit is told as a death: in V.7 Part II The Captain went along the wall, and at each True Man’s shoulder gave the call: “Shift your ground.” He hurried not, as lamplighters hurry not, lest the taper die. / Each battery he had reached lived. One at a time is slow. Pellow’s was the farthest. It is what the player feels when one battery can be commanded at a time while the rest fight on their own.
  • The runners carry what is seen, never commands, to wherever the Captain stands (§2.2, J7). Their words tell him where to walk.
  • The law, told as character. Kael’s mend states determinism: “It is not the world that sloweth, lad. It is the Captain who will not hurry. The moment he chose was the moment that came.” Strategy, not skill.
  • How the Book names it (changed in v3). Only in I.3, and only as something men say, never as a rule: In the laying the hours go slow, for stone is heavy, and in the hauling slower. In the holding an hour is gone in a heartbeat.; “For the Captain, an hour lasteth as long as a day.”; and Hale counting the ripples down a sail, Each, I think, was a minute. v2.0’s A day has four-and-twenty hours in it is gone with the rest of the arithmetic. The Book gives no numbers and names no mechanic. No text of any kind appears during a live Fight.
  • The battery and the gun. A battery is a group of guns, not one gun (admiral doc §12). Twelve True Men matches the late cap of twelve guns, so no battery lacks a True Man even if every gun stood alone; that is a fit, not a rule, and the Book states no number of guns. In the first year there is one battery, Kael’s guns, that are yet all we have.

5.12 · Other surfaces, and the art hooks

  • Personas. Ask Seren (not Ask the Emissary: the murdered Guest must not share a title with a helper). Ask Halyna answers in two voices, a line each, and says we two, never Halyna. Recommended: a persona answers in the register of the player’s tab (§7.2 call 19).
  • The lore screen is Open the Book.
  • Store art and screenshots use the Title of Liberty, drawn in the Captain’s coal hand with the English beneath. Never the last line of the game, never the sliver’s sign, never We remember or We remember our home too.
  • Achievements. Halyna’s Blessing (not Rhyna’s). Pacifist may add one line, never a leaf: “Once the wall held and not one hull was sunk. Halyna said nothing for a long while.” The rungs that translate the native hands are in §5.7.
  • Campaign cards. The 330 quotes and the soldier roster are recast in a separate pass, in the cadence of the stone leaves, shortened for a card.
  • Dispatches. Between sorties, on the Deploy map’s state-read beat (admiral doc §9c), never during timed Build or a live Fight, one line may tell what the fleet learned of the player in that siege, in a lookout’s voice or Halyna’s, from the fleet’s own record: “They marked that the north holds its fire.” · “They know we move the guns they find.” · “They know we kill the bearers first.” A dispatch says only what the fleet has learned, never what it will do, and it forgets with the siege. It is what is seen, as a runner carries it.
  • Art hooks.
    • The grown hulls are grey-green; every keep flies a crimson pennant, the colour of the torn cloak (Crimson is the pennant on this Keep, and on every keep we raise.); the answering sail blanches pale, grey-green to white, like a man’s face.
    • The root-men are bound bundles of grey-green living root that walk and knot together in fours (Off the transports the root-men came like spilled kindling.). The crown-hulls are broad hulls whose crown opens; the winged seed has membranous wings; the husks fly with it, paler and empty; the great seed rides alone under its own bladder.
    • The Captain’s half-cloak, by stage: to the knees at first launch; shorter by a hand’s breadth at each wall death (Cam 2, 9, 16, 25, 29, 32 complete, Rimewatch, the Epilogue; the Rimewatch strip is §7.2 call 3); at Count VI it cannot reach his belt; the last strip is the last of his half, whatever remains; after it, a plain grey cloak.
    • The Captain’s hood: up in every hearth scene; back only at IV.3’s interstitial and at the Epilogue (§5.4).
    • Crimson strips on pikes: over the east cairns (Jory, Corlen, Wick, Pellow), on the east face over Ebba, over the north stone (Enrella), over the mere at Rimewatch (Hesk, by the call), and over the inner gate (Halyna).
    • The north lamp on the north wall, lit every dusk: by Hesk, then by the fisher-girl of the east keep.
    • Two figures at every Stonewright post, working as one; look again. Ye will see two.
    • Each battery’s lookout with one of Pellow’s far-glasses.
    • The runners’ stone in the ward, a fallen block by the inner gate.
    • The tale-stone in its niche of the hearth-wall, face down, with hands on it.
    • The sending boat’s keyed stem, the two halves of the first heart, with Halyna’s mark across the join; the green sliver on moss in the bow, with the coin and the two chisels.
    • The staff in the gatehouse corner, under its dust; at the end, a clean stroke in the dust where it stood.
    • The three grey stones over the Long Hearth after the homecomings, the lowest scarred by old pipes.
    • The inner gate standing open at night after the seventh spring.

The builder’s gaps (from the v3 editor’s log, for wf10/book/build_story_html.py and story.css)
  • it stops on <!-- READING --> as an unknown marker;
  • it reads the first italic paragraph after a title as the teller line, so the v3 dateline is taken for the headnote;
  • <!-- HALYNA --> sets data-told="halyna" on the unit, which story.css does not style, so it only inks the halves a and b; the split pairs outside a cue need the markers, which v3 now gives them;
  • it must align the tabs by the shared anchors (§5.8), not by paragraph;
  • IV.2’s ::: note English should show collapsed until the leaf has been read (w01’s request).

The token completes the sentence All that day lay the tenth Throne where it fell, black of grain and split open, a hill of timber higher than a harbour-house. It was {LAST_THRONE_AT_HAVEN}. The wood’s things are written by their own names (the foreword), so the token gives the true name and the place, never v1.3’s English name or a bare code:

5.14 · Ripples into other documents

  • The admiral doc §12, and the Legends delivery references in it: by horn becomes by his own voice, at the battery’s side; the runners are his eyes. Its links to v1.3’s III.2 (the Thrones) go to VI.2.
  • The delivery map’s own references (v1.3 §7 and the §9 batteries row) are superseded by §5.11 and §6.
  • The Original (The Legends, in Their Own Hands) must be set down anew from v3, every leaf (K2). Its I.3 (lo et gomm, the horn) and the lexicon’s gommharm (“a horn-call”) go; the lexicon already has movenn, “a banner, a sign flown”, and the Tongues team chooses the words for the Captain’s call at the battery’s side. The grain of every wood leaf is set ring by ring; the readings are Halyna’s English pieces and take no grain of their own.
  • MAP §4.1: the Captain’s Stonwryt under the new capstone becomes Halyna’s mark and the Title. The marks a player can match are added (§5.6).
  • The Tongues doc §2.8 call-out, revised: item 1 (the Fore on the chest lid) is replaced by Aldwena’s Stonwryt (if its drawing echoes the Fore’s shape, that is a hint only, and never a holy sign); item 3 is dropped; item 4’s lines must not say first tongue (J25). No pair’s mark is drawn as a cross. The Tongues doc’s links into the old delivery map move as §5.0 says.
  • The battle-message lord’s line: “My fathers sold their black pillars by the cartload…” (§5.10).
  • The Fiction Hooks still carry the old Throne and root names.
  • The UI: Ask Halyna; Halyna’s Blessing; Open the Book.
  • Plain Words is wf13/plain_v31.md (v3.1, §1.16; v3.0’s was wf11/plain_v3.md), the second tab inside the story page. The separate The Legends, in Plain Words (v1.3) and wf10/plain_v2.md are superseded.
  • The builders: the story builder must learn the v3 frame (§5.13); the Notes builder (wf10/notes_build/build_notes_html.py) must take this document’s new section numbers.
  • The working guides: wf11/voice_v3.md §4 quotes some lines in forms the Book later changed (Corlen’s has, Hesk’s has to, the ship-masters’ The shore laughs and comes for you when you die), and wf11/craft.md quotes “Hull hangs back.” and “Wait. The sea has not finished.” The Book’s forms win (§1.2); if the guides are used again, they should follow it.
  • The outline (wf9/outline.md): the lines §1.7 lists are retired.
  • The art list: the half-cloak by stage, the strips, the hood up, the north lamp and the fisher-girl, the stem, the lookouts, the tale-stone, the grey cloak (§5.12).

6The names

How the Book holds each name. “The Book” here is v3.0.0. Names marked “NOTES only” are never said in a tale; v3 moved several more there (§2.4).

The peoples and the land

NameWhat it isHow it is used
the Shorelanders · the Rivenmenthe people, before and after the NamingOur fathers we call Shorelanders, for so they were, and ourselves but we. (I.5). The wood calls them the shore-men, and the late carvings Rhenear.
the Shorelands · Rivenkeepthe land; the hold on its ridgeEvery haven the grey sails hold is Rivenkeep, and the edge of the sea is the edge of the Keep. It is a vow. (I.5). The eldest murmur “the Shorelands” into their shawls yet.
the ten havensEldhythe (the Eldest Haven), Tidesmeet (where the river meeteth the tide), Fenholm (the town that was always sinking), Holtward (the keep under the canopy), Carnhold (the stronghold of the cairns), Emberhythe (the forge on the burning shore), Sandreach (the citadel carved in the canyon wall), Rimewatch (the watch on the frozen mere), Highreach (the tower of the floating stones), Glasspire (the last and proudest)Each epithet stands at its haven’s head in III.1. A haven need not stand on a harbour (Ulden’s line; the reason is NOTES only). v1.3’s the ten harbour-cities is no longer said in the Book.
the Mystaerithe people behind the greythe Rivenmen’s word: So we called it the ghost fleet, and its senders the Mystaeri, after the mist. (III.2). Their own name, Naelear, those of the breath, is first given in VI.3 (Mystaeri is but the word of the shore.) and then fills the untold word of II.2. Never Mystborn.
the Captain · the Commanderthe player, never namedThe Rite names him Commander of Rivenkeep (I.4) and the wall uses the title; In his own heart he was still only the Captain. v2.0’s the Captain of the Torn Cloak is no longer said in the Book.
the wardenthe master of the outpostnever commander; his name is always ▒▒▒▒ in stone; in wood he is the master of that house, the host.
the Trade Councilthe havens’ governing merchantssent out the axes; shelved the warning; kept the letter; the Council named it fortune; the warden held the post by his father’s name on the Trade Council.

The wood

NameWhat it isHow it is used
the wood · the Mystwoodtheir living wood: twisted boughs for rites, pale saplings for song, silver elders for council, black pillars at the edgeThe Book says the wood. Mystwood stands only in Jack’s line, But Mystwood remembers. (IV.5). It is not read; it is known. One life in two kinds: From the first the long-lived guided the wood, and the wood breathed out the sky they lived by.
the Mystholders · the Naelsaenthe black pillars at the fog’s edge, the anchors of the mistMystholders is the ship-masters’ name (IV.1). The wood says our pillars and the Naelsaen, the mist-hearts: the anchors of our sky (II.2). They regrew after the felling until the cutting of the regrowth made them stop; that is why the mist thinned.
the grey · the fog · the breath · the Mystlandsthe living mistThe Rivenmen say the grey and the fog; Elspet says the Mystlands (Jack’s own word, kept; IV.5). The wood says the breath.
the silver eldersthe council-treesthe wood’s name: none lie beneath a silver elder, save the Aelvaren’s keel among their roots. Silverbark, the wall’s grain-name, is NOTES only now.
Ael’tharthe holiest rite of fellowship: gift, blood and browIn the thunder of his own tongue he spake the name of the rite, Ael’thar (IV.4). The rite is told as it happens: the gift set down kneeling; the hand on the neck and the foreheads together; a shallow cut on the host’s arm, and then on the guest’s own; the arms laid together, that harm to the one should be harm to both. The soft carved form Aelthar is NOTES only now.
aelthe joining (NOTES only)the one hint of the two tongues’ kinship to reach the hearth (V.7 Part I), and v3 no longer glosses it. Nothing more is said (J25).
the Aelvaren, the Bearer of the Bindingthe Guest’s boatproud of the name (IV.6); the ship-masters’ burning boat (IV.5); the sending turns as it turned (VI.3).
Aelrhen · the Guestthe emissaryAelrhen he was named when he took his errand: bond-to-stone. (IV.4). Brenn says the stranger. The Rivenmen say the Guest and the grey man of the old tale. v2.0’s the Kneeling Guest is no longer said in the Book.
the Mystarchs · the Thronesthe ten eldest pillars, grown into thrones and seated in the ten fallen havensThe wall named them Mystarchs, and the soldiers the Thrones. (V.5, Count VI); Ebba says the last Mystarch (I.5). Each has a fear-name (NOTES and the game only), an English name the wall used at its homecoming, and a true name (below).
the long-lived · the children of the long-livedthe Mystaeri, in the wood’s mouththe wood’s own words for them.
the council · the grove · the growingthe council under the silver elders; the vote; the fleetsNo roof it had but the boughs, nor floor but the moss. (V.4). The Sending is these notes’ name for VI.3’s deed.
the bond of rootshow the heart’s orders go to every hullV.6: Through the bond of roots we carved them on into every hull about us, and every hull obeyed. · The long-lived made that bond into a leash. Its wood name, Aelralen, is NOTES only now.
the Last Carverthe last carver in the grovesSeren’s name for him: The Last Carver I call him, for I think that when he cut this there was no other. He never sailed. His fate is untold.
Rhenearthe stone-deaf, the late carvings’ name for the RivenmenV.7 Part II: Small in that carving was cut their word for us. It shares -ear, those of, with Naelear.
Myststonestone that was woodthe Epilogue: Myststone the Rivenmen call it, and say it remembereth all. Stone does not translate still holds for every other stone.

  • The grains, in the wall’s names and in the order of their tempers (NOTES only; V.6 keeps the tempers as griefs, with no names): the green wood (too young to have a way), Ironbark (comes again heavier where it failed), Willow (turns from the first failure), Blackthorn (goes where it lost the most), Heartoak (never turns aside), Twistbough (plays no trick twice), Yew (waits, and burns, and waits again), Ashwood (throws all it has at once), Silverbark (knows you best, and bends least). The tempers as tells are mechanics, and live in the fleet design.

The fleet, as the wall names it

NameWhat it isHow it is used
the grey sails · the ghost fleet · the drumsthe soldiers’ words for what they sawNo one stands on the decks and no one is aboard (No one saileth in us., V.6). The drums are the knocking out of the grey, like thunder, on and on. A sail blanches pale when its hull wakes to another’s deed.
the Standard-Bearer · the bearer · the heartthe fleet’s chain of command: the bearer hull, the heart in it that carves each morrow, the bond that carries it to every hullWhen we sank a bearer, another took up the standard, and the fleet took on the new bearer’s temper. (Count II). Sethvaren and Saenvael are NOTES only. The fleet design calls the carving heart the Heartwood and its orders carvings. The heart is not a person: the ship is the commander.
the root-men · treelingsthe warriors of the landings, grown of rootRoot-men is the Book’s word; treelings is the wall’s slang (Treelings the wall called them, as if a name could make them small.; battle messages). What those roots touched, the wood knew. They were the wood’s hands.
the crown-hulls · the winged seed · a seed-fallthe carriers, their bombers, a bombing runThen came the crown-hulls, and their winged seed, to wherever our guns were quiet. (Count II). The husks are NOTES and art only now.
the tabletsthe soldiers’ word for the planks in the vaultThe wall called them the tablets. They were but planks. (V.2)

The guild and the bond

NameWhat it isHow it is used
the Stonewrightsthe guild of master-builders and keepers of the faithBuild, do not destroy. Protect, do not attack. No Stonewright ever bore a weapon.
the Stonwryt · the Stonwryt coineach bonded pair’s own sacred mark; the coin struck under master-pairs’ marks, part gold and part black stoneThe Stonwryt the masters call it: a vow sealed in living rock, earned by toil and skill · trust, sealed in stone (II.1). Never a holy sign. The coin goes into the sending boat (VI.3).
the Aetherbond · the Bondedthe joining of two Stonewrights, made by God before birth, read in the cradle, made whole at the sealingthe Bonded, capitalised, is the guild’s name for every bonded pair; the adjective stays lower case (a bonded pair). When the left hand dies, does not the right one die too? (II.3).
the sealing · the pair-namethe wedding of a bonded pair, when the guild gives a name for the mouth, and a mark for the stoneSix pair-names: Halyna, Aldwena, Idrenna, Orvenna, Enrella, Wendhessa. A pair-name stands under a lintel on the page and always takes a plural verb (§2.2, J2).
Halyna · Halvard Stone-Warden · Rhyna of the Inner Gate · the Bonded of the Keep · Elder and Elderessthe elders of the guild at the Keep, husband and wifethe pair-name is the guild’s and the hearth’s; in a scene the cradle-names do the work. Elder Halvard am I, and she is Elderess Rhyna, and neither is the elder of the other. (II.3).
the unbonded · the thirda guild child whose cradle-mate died before the sealing, given to a bonded hearth as its thirdThe unbonded keep one name, and among the guild one name is a mourning. The unbonded have no mark.
Seren Two-Inks · Tarnel · Tarnardthe scribe of the Book, Halyna’s third; her cradle-mate, a boatwright’s son of Tidesmeet; his father, the eldest of the boatwrightsSeren means a single sorrow that overcomes (NOTES only). Two-Inks for the two inks she keeps for Halyna, and in the Epilogue she writes in one ink. Tarnel means little net (NOTES only).
the Theoliths · the Covenant of Completionthe old master-pairs who teach; the vow they name to childrenHalyna’s Theoliths are Idrenna. God taketh not the one without leaving the other a work to do.
the Knowing · a knowing · the Book of KnowingsHalyna’s rite of touching the wreck-wood; what the wood gives; the appendixa knowing is given whole, as memory; the telling is the hard part, and Halyna give it in pieces. The Knowing is also the debrief line’s in-world name. Anything read with the eyes (the Title, the slates, the rolls, a root sign, the Stone) is still read.
the readingHalyna’s pieces at the head of a wood leafthese notes’ name for the <!-- READING --> block (§1.5); the Book never names it.

The wall, its frame and its things

NameWhat it isHow it is used
the True Men · the Twelvethe Captain’s company, each the leader of a troop and of a batteryWe kept our oaths, and built what we promised, and fought never but in a just cause. That is the whole of a True Man. (I.1). The roll: Stannard of the ridge; Corlen of the harbour; Tamm of the three spans; Marl the fen-born; Aske of the canopy; Garvel of the smithies; Harl of the burning shore; Ulden of the galleries; Hesk of the mere; Lanner of the crags; Pellow of the spire; and Kael the Counter, their oath-keeper, of no haven. Raised later: Voss who asked (Corlen’s battery) and Ruan of the spire (Pellow’s). fourteen names

the runners · the runners’ oath · the runners’ stonethe men who carry what is seen; their oath; the fallen block in the wardWhat I saw, I carry. I carry nothing else., and from IV.3, Not as Brenn carried. Every haven had its runners’ stone. Hale and Wick are runners, not True Men.
the six companiesthe soldiers who walked the long retreatsoldiers of six broken companies, that knew their officers no more; a troop to each of us (I.1). Voss was a corporal of them.
the Long Hearth · the tale-stone · laid · We rememberthe framethe tale-stone is the old capstone of the inner gate in its niche. A tale is laid, not told: A tale that is but told is gone by morning. A tale that is laid is part of the wall. The answer comes after every stone leaf, and after a wood leaf first in VI.3. Tumar, the Orrowen for We remember, is also how Orrowen says yes (NOTES only).
the banner · the torn cloak · the half-cloakthe written half on its pike; the unwritten half on the Captain’s backthe banner is the hinge of the clock. The strips are cut from the half-cloak (§3.8).

the founders’ leaf · the youngest apprentice’s leaf · the slatesAldwena’s leaf of the raising; the leaf of the hearth’s laying of tales; the Rite and the slates of building for battleall from the black chest; all in the Hal. v2.0’s the hearth leaf is these notes’ name only.
the staff · the staffsthe first staff, capped with the finest polished ashlar; the havens’ staffsthe law of the staff (§2.2, J10).
granite-bloom · bloomthe pale crust on worked stone untended for lives of menBloom the masons call the pale crust…, for it flowereth where none come.
the east cairns · the north stone · the north lampthe Keep’s burial ground on the east face; Enrella’s grave in the north breach; Hesk’s lamp, then the fisher-girl’sthe rites of mourning (§3.8).
the lintel of the western stairwhere the quarry-wardens cut their names, one beneath anotherKael swears by it (I.1). Halvard’s is the last name, cut by Halyna (the Epilogue).
the vaultthe oldest room under this hall, where the Rite was foundwhere the planks lie, and whoso will may go down and lay a hand on them (V.2).

Originality

Jack, 2026-09-30: “No more name or word checks are necessary. Overlap is just overlap now.” No name or word audit was run for v3, and none is owed. Only an unmistakable trademark lift would be flagged. The flags v2.0 recorded are kept here for the record and need no action: the Ancient Treant and the Frost Wyrm (game fear-names only now, and still a call for Jack, §7.2 call 4); Highreach and Sandreach; Kael beside Ael’thar; and Orvenna, a soft flag only.

7Settled and still-open calls

7.1 · Settled

Jack’s notes of 2026-10-02, as applied in v3.0.0

  • K1 to K9, as §1.1 records them, and the memory theme (§1.9).
  • The register: the v1.3 voice and sentence-craft, made hard by heavy old English, never by length (§1.2). The 2026-09-30 note on long joined sentences is superseded for sentence length, and so is writing for the voice.
  • The custom of burning is gone from the Book and from these notes; the burning is the warden’s order, to hide the deed (§1.7, §1.8).
  • Brenn only watched (§1.7).
  • Plain Words is a true retelling (§1.10).
  • One switch for the tabs; the Original back in every section; the shared copy is the whole story (§1.11).

The calibration’s rulings (§1.2; voice_v3.md §9), which the writers inherited:

  • Register A, HEAVY, by the judges’ votes A 3, B 1, C 0. B (cosmetic archaism) and C (native words in the prose) are declined (§7.3).
  • One past form to a verb, Book-wide; wist for a fact; mine before a vowel or h; one -eth to a sentence at most; plain words at the hinge.
  • The True Men’s refrains are under the dial (Wait. The sea hath not finished. · Someone must keep a light.); fixed matter is outside it; the three calls are never put into thou; the warden’s letter is the one modern register in the Book.
  • The burning is the warden’s order, Leave naught to show, and the rhyme keeps his words; Tobe says nothing.
  • The turn keeps IV.5’s lock: the boat comes with no wind and goes Into the wind … and against the current. Which no boat can do is the ship-masters’ wonder in IV.5, not Brenn’s.
  • The Guest’s own cut is seen in IV.3 and named only in IV.4. The wood’s scream is literal.
  • I.1’s roll stays bare, and each man’s tag lives whole in III.1 (all eleven are carried there).
  • Ebba’s first We remember and Idrenna’s leave to break Wendhessa’s seal moved to the foreword. I.1’s loaf picture is left out.

Settled from Notes v1.0.0’s open calls

  • The Invocation’s register (v1.0.0 call 1). It follows the Book: heavy grammar, every picture and key phrase kept (Whoso holdeth it now is the teller. · the building and the breaking · we have kept both · Hold the stone. Lay the tale. When it is laid, the hearth will answer.), with Many hands have held it, and many shall. new. Plain Words tells it plainly from v3’s own Invocation. v1.3’s wording stays on record as the fallback: Sit, if there is room, and stand if there is not. The guns are cooling, and the mortar will not set before dark. / Here is the hearth, and here is the stone that fell from the inner gate. Whoever holds it tells. / We tell these tales because we forgot once. Our fathers forgot a keep, and it crumbled. Their fathers forgot a wrong, and it came back to us under grey sails. So we tell all of it: the building and the breaking, and what we did as well as what was done to us. / Some of these tales are ours, and they are cut plain, like the stones of the wall. Some are the wood’s, and those are hard to tell, and will not be hurried. Where the two do not agree, we have kept both. / Hold the stone. Lay the tale. When it is laid, the hearth will answer.
  • Length (call 2): K6 answers it; every leaf is under its ceiling (§1.3).
  • More impact moments than the outline’s one to three (call 3): one halt to a tale, with its strike lines (§1.4).
  • III.2’s two added lines (call 7): gone from the Book.
  • The prologue’s length (call 14): about 300 words; paced by beats; no fallback cut needed (§5.2).
  • Kael’s opening oath (call 12): it ends What I told not, that will I not tell., on one syllable, as his other oaths do.
  • The [may] calls v2.0 took, kept in v3: the cradle-rhyme; Voss’s Child have I none of mine own, and yet I said our; Kael’s I have no haven; Harl carrying the bow to the tide; the Burn plank laid on the black crust at Emberhythe; Two at the Gate sung that night with the first voice silent; the inner gate standing open at night; the north lamp lit by the child.
  • The [may] call v2.0 took and v3 dropped: the soldier’s brand quenched in the sea (K8; §1.8).
  • The two children (R6b’s open question 4): one child. The fisher-girl of the east keep, Ebba’s great-granddaughter, lights the north lamp in VI.1, answers the wood in VI.3, lays the first hand on the boat that came back, and keeps the light at the Epilogue’s close. The small girl of II.3 is another child, unnamed, and needs no payoff.
  • Tobe (R6b’s open question 3): kept, for the poles and his thirty years, with no saying.
  • The Epilogue’s fire beat (R6b’s open question 2): dropped.
  • The wood leaves’ form (R6b’s open question 1): Halyna’s reading, then Seren’s story in the wood’s we (§1.5).
  • Brenn’s line from Jack’s own words (R6b’s open question 5): I did not stop them. A boy was I among the men.; the youngest of the twelve is not added beside Nineteen I was.
  • Hold the stone, Seren. is bare Book-wide; its whole form is said once (I.4).
  • The split pairs: every pair of Halyna’s halves is inside a HALYNA block wherever it stands; other pairs’ halves stay in the leaf’s ink; the Epilogue’s remembered halves stay unwrapped, in one ink.

Jack’s notes of 2026-09-30, as v2.0 applied them and v3 keeps or supersedes them (§2).

Jack’s ruling of 2026-09-30 on originality: “No more name or word checks are necessary. Overlap is just overlap now.” No audit is run or owed (§6).

Jack’s answers of 2026-09-27, still standing

  • Three tabs at every tale and at the foreword, the Invocation, the Epilogue and the Book of Knowings: The Book, Plain Words, Original (§5.8); and now one switch for all of them (K1).
  • Tier 3: the whole Book set down in its own tongues (to be done anew from v3; K2).
  • Seren means a single sorrow that overcomes.
  • The Latin cross is fine as the Bar Before’s drawn shape, and both tongues may come from one first tongue; J25 adds that the kinship is only hinted and that no cross-sign is ever a holy sign.
  • Tumar, We remember, is Orrowen’s yes.
  • The chisel’s economy and the grain’s rings belong to the Tongues doc.

Jack’s answers of 2026-09-26, still standing

  • The sending is kept and seaworthy: Tarnard lays the keel and the boatwrights build it of the vault’s planks, Orvenna key the stem, Halyna cut their mark across it, and A day it rode off the point in a hard swell, and took no water. (VI.3).
  • The Bonded’s death together is kept (II.3, V.7 Part I, the Epilogue).
  • “The stone-deaf” is kept, as Rhenear (V.7 Part II).
  • The Last Carver is kept, and he never sails.
  • The proposed names are kept, with Naelear spelled Jack’s way. Who are the unbonded? is answered in II.3.
  • The Whispers were given free rein (§5.9).
  • The smaller calls: the torn cloak and every pennant are crimson; the Mystaeri live two and a half times as long as the Shorelanders; the UI currency sounds like Stonewright when spoken.
  • The assumed rulings: the Thrones were not in the Fall (Late came we. It was not we that burned your havens.); no Mystaeri sail; Myststone is stone that was wood; the battles are fought along the line of keeps; Rhyna’s cry whole; the dead heart’s hour late; But Mystwood remembers in Voss’s mouth; there is no admiral and no one aboard.
  • The Mystlands kept as Jack’s own word; faith is never the butt of a joke.

7.2 · Still open, for Jack (each reversible)

Each is applied in the Book or in these notes as recommended, and each can be undone in a line or a table row.

Call 1

Brenn’s laughter. v1.3 and v2.0 had I laughed. I have not laughed at anything since. K7 says he only watched, so v3 reads Then laughed his men. I stood among them, and I hear that laughter yet., and later, of the axemen, I laughed not, for I had seen it turn. Yet I said naught. Recommended: as the Book has it. Fallback (voice §9.1): v1.3’s line returns, but only as a boy’s echo of the men’s laughter, and the axemen line (I laughed not…) changes to match.

Call 2

Sharing before the Original is set down (K2, K3). The Original is translated, merged and on the story page (§1.15, §1.16). Recommended: share the whole story page now, with The Book and Plain Words whole and the Original tab carrying every native drawing the Book holds, at its place; republish the same page as each leaf’s Orrowen and grain are set down (the Book only adds). Alternative: wait until the Tongues pass has set the whole Book down.

Call 3

Hesk’s strip. v3’s VI.1 ends Rimewatch on He came home., and the strip that v2.0 told after it was cut, so the Book tells seven strips. Recommended: the art still shows a strip over the mere, and §3.8 counts eight; nothing in the Book contradicts it, and It was the last of his half. gives no number. Alternatives: restore v2.0’s sentence after He came home., which fits the v3 grammar as it stands: That night the Captain cut a hand’s breadth from his half of the cloak, and bound it on a pike over the mere. (VI.1 has 50 words of room under its ceiling); or show seven strips in the art.

Call 4

The fear-names the Ancient Treant and the Frost Wyrm are now game words only, in no leaf. Recommended: keep them, under Jack’s ruling that overlap is just overlap. Alternatives (from v2.0): the Root-Giant and the Rime-Serpent, one word each in the battle messages.

Call 5

VI.1 calls each Throne by the English of its own name (the Tide-Heart, the Bearer of New Branches…) and keeps the soldiers’ fear-names out of the Book. The alternative is the fear-names in VI.1.

Call 6

VI.1 is laid at each haven’s own fire, on the night its Throne fell, with Kael’s frame at the Long Hearth when the tenth was home. Fallback: lay every entry at the Long Hearth, recast the headnote’s first sentence to say so, and change Voss’s They are in my pack tonight to I have carried them up the mountain.

Call 7

VI.2’s Eirlenth: At the last a small fire stood over us, where the hall had been. It is Hesk’s lamp, felt from under the ice, and it names nothing. The death ledger lists no VI.2 line for Hesk; it can be struck alone.

Call 8

Halyna cut Halvard’s name in the lintel of the western stair, together, ere their chisels went into the grey (the Epilogue). v2.0 had Halvard cut it himself. Another alternative: Stannard cuts it after their death.

Call 9

The new verses, for Jack’s nod: the masons’ work-chant (I.2); the True Men’s dawn drill, with the three calls word for word (I.3); Rhyna’s mother’s cradle-rhyme (I.4, four lines).

Call 10

thee in “Hold it, and it will hold thee.” (I.2 and the Epilogue): thou to one near hearer, a frightened child. If Jack holds the line fixed as you, it reverts in one word in both leaves. The Plain keeps you.

Call 11

Kael’s sloweth and the wall’s lasteth (I.3). Jack loved v1.3’s “It is not the world that slows, lad.” The voice rule (no -s beside -eth in one mouth) made it sloweth. Revert both to v1.3’s words if he prefers them.

Call 12

The Captain standeth still. (I.3) is Hale’s slip into the present at the run, at a strike line, where the hinge rule would want shared words. If the hinge rule should govern, it becomes The Captain stood still., and the run’s present ends a sentence sooner.

Call 13

Idrenna’s prayer for the dead begins “God, thou madest these two one ere either drew breath.” (no Christian mark: Lord is gone), and is told whole once, over Enrella (V.7 Part I); at Ebba’s grave it is told without its words, and the Epilogue no longer quotes it.

Call 14

The ship-masters’ fourth saying is an imperative: Let the shore laugh, for the shore will never have to meet it. The voice guide had The shore laughs…; in the Book’s grammar that would be The shore laugheth….

Call 15

Small stagings the writers added inside the elements’ frame, each reversible: the gull at the warden’s door; Tobe’s thirty years, and Tobe washing the poles; the boy at the front who counts twelve on his fingers; Seren singing to the comma, and then … the rest; the Captain coming in off the wall at his own Rite; the masons laying on below at Hold; Tamm’s stones and Aske’s hand on the wreck-wood at the hearth; the tellers laying hands on the stone in turn (III.2); Wick swearing the oath loudest; Merrick by the door, and he is not gone out; the first pillar, that had marked the most of your hulls; the tide that came in under the Aelvaren, and turned, and he came not; the one boat that came farther than any (II.2); Stannard carrying Enno the mile; Kael counting Hesk’s steps to two hundred and twelve; Harl bearing the first plank to Tarnard’s feet; the fisher-girl to her waist in the sea; Seren reading the Stone out to the children; no child laughed. Strike any Jack does not want.

Call 16

The lines that left the Book (§1.14). Any can return in one edit.

Call 17

The foreword at first launch (§5.2). Recommended: keep it in the codex from the start, as v1.3 and v2.0 did. Its costs: a player can read the dead teller that held up a wet finger to every wind as Corlen from Cam 5, four campaigns before he dies; it shows Enrella alive; and it tells that the seal was broken once the wood had spoken. Alternative: hold it until Cam 20, when Seren writes it in the third summer, which spares all three but leaves the reader without the frame’s key for twenty campaigns.

Call 18

I.3 opens from Cam 2 (§5.3), so the play order matches the hearth’s. Fallback: v1.3’s Cam 1 gate.

Call 19

The hearth-words, Whispers and personas follow the reader’s tab new: the Book’s words under The Book, the Plain’s under Plain Words. Recommended, since K1 makes the tab one choice for the whole Book. The alternative is the Book’s words everywhere.

Call 20

The token against VI.1’s fates. VI.3 has the tenth Throne lying split open, and Halyna go in; VI.1 has Thrones sink, crumble or crust over. It is v2.0’s premise and a game-wide rule, left as it was. The fix, if wanted, is a fate-clause in the token or a VI.1 allowance that the last one fell where a hand could go in.

Call 21

Smaller points kept as they stand:

  • Rhyna says So were we two sealed, and named Halyna. once in II.3, as a name received, against the rule that Halyna never name themselves.
  • Two at the Gate is the old gate-song of the havens, yet it names grey sails, which no haven had seen before the Fall. It is kept verbatim (J5); the hearth can hear it as a song the masons made new in the Fall.
  • Corlen did what the Captain would have done names Corlen, so the antecedent is clear.
  • Tarnel’s death is told by what it left (She woke beside him. · Tarnel did not wake.). If Jack wants it said outright: The cold took him.
  • Naelsaen, the mist-hearts stands in the clear in II.2 before VI.3 tells Naelear: a seed, not a spoiler.
  • II.2 and IV.4 both open their headnotes on a child’s question.
  • Tidesmeet, where the river meeteth the tide: the epithet takes the -eth. If Jack rules -s forms for epithets, it is one word.
  • Idlan and Denna are named nowhere in the Book. If Jack wants them, their place is one clause in the foreword.
Call 22

One word in a Whisper. Jack’s GDD line “The fleet cannot be recalled.” is, in Count VI, “The fleet cannot be called home,” / “for the wood obeyeth the wood.”, to play against V.6’s No voice among us can call us home. The Whisper follows the Book (§5.9). Restore recalled in the Whisper if Jack prefers his words.

Call 23

v1.3’s open calls that are still open:

  • Seren’s epithet, Seren Two-Inks; fallback Seren who writes, to match Voss who asked.
  • Root-men or treelings as the Book’s word (now root-men, with treelings the wall’s slang). A one-word swap.
  • Rhenear is respelled to follow Naelear; restore the old spelling if Jack meant only the one word.
  • The theaters: seven fit; Forest, Mountain, Desert and Frozen need only a skin fix; Sky and Crystal need a new premise under the same mechanic; Volcanic needs a light touch. One rule would settle the rest: every theater’s waterway begins at the sea.
  • The native hands: the leaf facing I.1 uncut until the Epilogue (recommended); the Stone’s telling of the Title’s five things (Home. Kin. The one they kneel to. The open door. The stillness.); when Seren’s line translates (at V.1, recommended); the old hand at Cam 16 as The Bedrock Hand; the achievement names, and whether they show as achievements or only as the Knowing bands.
Call 24

The half-cloak’s arithmetic. Seven or eight hand’s breadths do not quite carry a half-cloak from the knees to nothing; the art can make the last strip whatever remains (It was the last of his half.). The Book no longer states the rule that defines the strips; §3.8 gives it (the wall’s dead laid at this hearth since the banner), and it leaves out the troop dead under the cairns, for the cloak could not hold them.

7.3 · Declined alternatives

Kept here so that no choice is lost. Each was weighed and not taken.

In v3

  • Register B (MEDIUM), inversions and ere with no old verbs: cosmetic, and its Plain would be re-punctuation again (one judge’s vote).
  • Register C (NATIVE), Orrowen words in the English prose: it muddied the scenes and added lore (no votes).
  • A custom of the coast, in any form, as the burning’s reason or as the ship-masters’ fear (K8). And v2.0’s earlier option, the burning as a message sent back into the grey, stays declined with it. The reason is the warden’s.
  • Brenn striking the Guest (K7).
  • The Epilogue’s fire beat kept, with a ship-master’s fear-cry for fire and the brand quenched (R6b’s option i): it taught the custom by another road.
  • V.5 laid after V.7, so the counts could not spend Part I: it would break the Contents, the V.5–V.6 spread and the dateline order. The counts were rewritten instead.
  • ye in Jack’s dust line (If ye doubt the tale…): fixed matter stays outside the dial.
  • Forgetting chose our fathers. for the foreword: in the Book’s object-first order it reads backwards. Forgetting came on our fathers, and none chose it.
  • The gloss the joining on ael: it did the reader’s thinking.
  • A richer Book Four Argument, What was felled at the edge, and who came to the door: the wrong, told from both sides of the grey.: the bare line spoils nothing and matches the Contents.
  • We will not lay it all on the Council. restored; and it was no runner’s fault at Enrella’s grave; Idlan and Denna named in the foreword or at the terms; the last Throne for Ebba’s the last Mystarch; Myststone moved after the reading; made into ornament for reduced to ornament; Look Close and The Longest Knowing as V.7’s part titles; seeding lest, scarce, whoso and whither where they would not fall naturally; and Seren’s verdict line in II.3 (Yet she can write it truer than we two can tell it.), for the tellers’ verdict is shown, never said.

From v2.0, still declined

  • The calling-pike and the battery cloths, read by far-glass, in place of the horn. The Captain’s own voice at the battery was simpler and made one battery at a time physical.
  • The ten strips of the cloak for the ten havens. One object that counts the dead without a number was chosen.
  • Idrenna dying in the Joined Tide, instead of Enrella. A young pair, planted over three years, lands harder.
  • The rolls in the masters’ packs, with two chests of records. One archive chest holds the rolls, the mute stones and the bundle.
  • A Council lord’s carved box as the myrrh’s source. The road’s dead tie the anointing to Tarnel and Wendhessa.
  • The Rite in the first spring, waiting for the bloom. The fourteenth day keeps Commander from the first battle.
  • Glasspire’s great glass as a second cause of the sending. The last elder’s death is cause enough; the glass stays an injury the wood remembers (VI.2).
  • Halyna reading Brenn in the first winter and holding it back. The seal and Idrenna’s leave lock the delay to the Stonwryt and give Idrenna an act.
  • The stone tellings set in measured verse lines. Jack chose prose.
  • Kael’s tells as a rule-rhyme; the Count of Winters end page; young Ebba as the fisher-girl’s name; the warden’s grandson at the hearth; the coal bleeding into the pike’s stone; a lost staff found at Glasspire.

Research first (R1 Tolkien, R2 Homer and Auerbach, R3 economy and the archaic register, R4 how a story builds, R5 the measured diagnosis, R6a and R6b the element inventory), then a judged calibration of three registers (votes A 3, B 1, C 0) and two binding guides, the voice and the craft. The whole Book rewritten in v1.3’s voice and sentence-craft, made hard by heavy old English (Early Modern grammar in every line, a small stock of old words, plain words at the hinge, no sentence over 30 words, no contractions). Every tale built as a story: a question by the hundredth word, the stakes, one halt, a turn, a cost, a closing picture; every death planted and told whole once. The frame made light: datelines of eight words or fewer, headnotes of fifty, the Hear how openings and the records after the end gone, the hood back twice, the strip as the growing refrain, two lines Added in Seren’s hand. The Book at 29,541 words leaf by leaf, against v2.0’s 50,976, every element kept. The wood leaves rebuilt as Halyna’s fragmentary reading (<!-- READING -->) and Seren’s story in the wood’s we. Seren’s craft and the tellers’ agreement shown, never stated. IV.3: Brenn only watched; the warden, a weak man, burned the boat to hide the deed; there is no custom of burning anywhere, and IV.5, V.1, V.2, VI.3 and the Epilogue are mended. Smaller changes: Halyna cut Halvard’s name; the True Men raise the new capstone; the fisher-girl of the east keep is one child, from the north lamp to the boat that came back; Tarnel a boatwright’s son; the Bearer of New Branches; The wall named them Mystarchs, and the soldiers the Thrones. Every song, Jack’s lines, the liturgy, the oath, the letter, the Guest’s sentence, the Title and every native key unchanged. The whole Book rewritten as epic prose for the voice, in a high register of long joined sentences, every song and fixed line verbatim.
